Q1Physics
⚠ The paper prints the electron's mass as "1/(4πε₀) = 9 × 10⁻³¹ kg": the label 1/(4πε₀) is misplaced and the value is the mass of the electron (m = 9 × 10⁻³¹ kg). Kept as printed. Four-file audit, 2026-11-04.
subjectPhysics
streamnot applicable (Physics has no streams)
branchnot applicable (Physics has no branches)
chapterModern Physics high confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on photon momentum p = E/c compared with electron momentum p = sqrt(2mE), the dual-nature/photon-momentum content taught in Modern Physics.
subtopic 1de Broglie Wavelength of Matter Waves
subtopic 2Photon Energy and Number of Photons
why subtopic 1The answer depends on the electron's momentum from its kinetic energy, p_e = sqrt(2mE), the matter-wave momentum relation taught with de Broglie waves, giving p_e/p_Ph = c*sqrt(2m/E) = 225.
why subtopic 2The question explicitly compares this with the photon's momentum p_Ph = E/c, which is the photon energy-momentum relation, so the photon topic is a genuine half of the comparison.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
A photon and an electron, each of 20 eV energy, move in free space. The ratio of linear momentum of electron pe to that of photon pPh, pepPh is : (Take speed of light = 3 × 108 m s-1, charge of electron = -1.6 × 10-19 C and mass of electron 14πε0 = 9 × 10-31 kg )
  1. 275
  2. 2450
  3. 1250
  4. 225
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (4)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 4 · high
  1. E = 20 eV = 20 × 1.6 × 10-19 = 3.2 × 10-18 J.
  2. For the electron (non-relativistic), pe = 2mE = 2 × 9 × 10-31 × 3.2 × 10-18 = 5.76 × 10-48 = 2.4 × 10-24 kg m s-1.
  3. For the photon, pPh = Ec = 3.2 × 10-183 × 108 = 1.067 × 10-26 kg m s-1.
  4. pepPh = 2.4 × 10-241.067 × 10-26 = 225, which is option 4.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
44 highagree
difficultyhard · 4 steps
stepsConvert 20 eV to joules: E = 20 × 1.6×10⁻¹⁹ = 3.2×10⁻¹⁸ J → Photon momentum p_ph = E/c = 3.2×10⁻¹⁸ / 3×10⁸ ≈ 1.07×10⁻²⁶ kg m s⁻¹ → Electron (non-relativistic) momentum p_e = √(2mE) = √(2 × 9×10⁻³¹ × 3.2×10⁻¹⁸) = 2.4×10⁻²⁴ → Form the ratio p_e/p_ph and keep it the way round the question asks
confusing options2 of 4 · options 2, 4
Option 4 is the ratio as asked, and option 2 is exactly its reciprocal written in disguise (2/450 = 1/225), so anyone who divides photon by electron lands on it without noticing; 275 and 1/250 are only near-miss arithmetic and have no clean route.
why it is trickyHard: four steps of execution (eV→J conversion, two momentum evaluations, a division across powers of ten) with a live rival on top, since 2/450 is the same number inverted and dressed up so it does not look like 1/225 on sight; the method is standard, but the length plus the disguised reciprocal is what costs candidates here.
the catchThe reciprocal distractor hidden as 2/450 rather than 1/225, plus the order-of-magnitude bookkeeping in dividing 2.4×10⁻²⁴ by ~1.07×10⁻²⁶; the stem also mislabels the electron mass as 1/4πε₀, which a careless reader may stall on.

Q2Physics
subjectPhysics
streamnot applicable (Physics has no streams)
branchnot applicable (Physics has no branches)
chapterMechanical Properties of Fluids high confidence
why this chapterThe flow rate follows from combining the equation of continuity with Bernoulli's equation for the given pressure difference and cross-sectional areas.
subtopic 1Equation of Continuity and Bernoulli's Equation
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer (400 cm^3/s) follows from combining A1v1 = A2v2 with Bernoulli's equation ΔP = ½ρ(v2² − v1²) for the horizontal constricted pipe.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Water flows in a streamline motion through a horizontal pipe of circular cross-section as shown in the figure. The pressure difference of water between P and Q is 15 N m-2. The area of cross-section at P and Q are 40 cm2 and 20 cm2, respectively. The rate of flow of water through the pipe, in cm3 s-1, is: [Take density of water = 1000 kg m-3]
  1. 400
  2. 100
  3. 200
  4. 300
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (1)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 1 · high
  1. Continuity: AP vP = AQ vQ ⇒ vQ = 4020 vP = 2vP.
  2. Bernoulli (horizontal pipe): PP - PQ = 12ρ (vQ2 - vP2) = 12ρ (3vP2).
  3. 15 = 12(1000)(3vP2) = 1500 vP2 ⇒ vP2 = 0.01.
  4. vP = 0.1 m s-1 = 10 cm s-1.
  5. Rate of flow = AP vP = 40 cm2 × 10 cm s-1 = 400 cm3 s-1, which is option 1.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
11 highagree
difficultyhard · 4 steps
stepsApply continuity: A_P v_P = A_Q v_Q, so v_Q = 2 v_P (areas 40 and 20 cm²). → Apply Bernoulli for a horizontal pipe: ΔP = ½ρ(v_Q² − v_P²) = ½ρ(3v_P²). → Convert to consistent SI: 15 = 500·3v_P² → v_P² = 0.01 → v_P = 0.1 m s⁻¹. → Compute volume flow rate A_P v_P = 40×10⁻⁴ × 0.1 m³ s⁻¹ and convert back to cm³ s⁻¹.
confusing options2 of 4 · options 1, 3
400 is the correct A_P v_P, while 200 is exactly what you get if you pair the computed speed with the narrow area (or slip one factor of two in the continuity relation), so the two differ only by the bookkeeping the candidate has to get right; 100 and 300 have no clean wrong route.
why it is trickyFour steps, and the question is longer to execute than to plan: continuity, Bernoulli with the 3v_P² combination, a cm²→m² conversion in the middle and a m³/s→cm³/s conversion at the end, with a genuine rival (200) sitting exactly one slip away from the key.
the catchTwo unit conversions in opposite directions plus the factor-of-two between the two sections — the classic NEET fluid-mechanics loss points — and the distractor 200 is precisely the result of using the wrong area at the last line.

Q3Physics
subjectPhysics
streamnot applicable (Physics has no streams)
branchnot applicable (Physics has no branches)
chapterRotational Motion high confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on the angular momentum of a rigid disc rotating about a fixed axis, which is the same about any point on that axis, so L_A/L_B = 1; this is rotational motion.
subtopic 1Problems on Angular Momentum
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer (ratio 1) depends on the fact that the angular momentum of a body in pure rotation with its centre of mass at rest is the same about every point, so L_A = L_B.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
A thin horizontal disc is rotating about a vertical axis passing through its fixed centre O. Its angular momentum is LA and LB computed about points A and B, respectively, with OB = 2 × OA. The value of LALB is :
  1. 2
  2. 14
  3. 12
  4. 1
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (4)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 4 · high
  1. The centre O (the centre of mass) is fixed, so →vcm = 0 and the total linear momentum →P = M→vcm = 0.
  2. About any point P: →LP = →LO + →rPO × →P.
  3. With →P = 0, →LA = →LB = →LO = Iω, independent of OA and OB.
  4. LALB = 1, which is option 4.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
44 highagree
difficultyhard · 2 steps
stepsRecall that for a rigid body, angular momentum about an arbitrary point P equals L_cm + M(rcm/P × v_cm). → Note the centre O is fixed, so v_cm = 0 and the extra term vanishes for both A and B, making L_A = L_B.
confusing options3 of 4 · options 2, 3, 4
A candidate who assumes angular momentum 'about a farther point' scales with separation lands on 1/2, and one who assumes an r² dependence (as with moment of inertia and the parallel-axis idea) lands on 1/4; both are exactly the results of the two commonest wrong routes, while the key 1 looks suspiciously trivial.
why it is trickyOnly two lines of work, but the whole question hinges on one unsignposted insight — that with a stationary centre of mass the translational term M r × v_cm is zero, so angular momentum is the same about every point — and the distractors 1/2 and 1/4 are precisely what the distance-scaling and r²-scaling misconceptions produce, so elimination gives no help.
the catchThe insight (v_cm = 0 kills the point-dependence of L) is not hinted at anywhere in the stem; students instinctively transfer the parallel-axis r² dependence of moment of inertia to angular momentum and the options reward that error directly.

Q4Physics
subjectPhysics

chapterElectromagnetic Induction high confidence
why this chapterThe answer is the self-inductance of a long solenoid, L = mu0 n^2 pi r^2 l, which is taught under self induction in electromagnetic induction.
subtopic 1Self Inductance of Solenoid
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer is the self-inductance formula of a long solenoid, L = mu0 pi n^2 r^2 l, which is taught under self inductance of a solenoid.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Consider a long solenoid of length l and radius r. If n is the number of turns per unit length and μ0 is the permeability of free space, the inductance of the solenoid is :
  1. 2μ0 π n2 r2 l
  2. μ0 π n2 r2 l
  3. μ0 n2 r2 l
  4. (μ0 / 2π) n2 r2 l
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (2)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · high
  1. Field inside a long solenoid: B = μ0 n I.
  2. Flux through one turn: φ = B A = μ0 n I π r2.
  3. Total turns N = n l, so flux linkage Nφ = μ0 n2 π r2 l  I.
  4. L = NφI = μ0 π n2 r2 l, which is option 2.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
22 highagree
difficultymedium · 1 step
stepsRecall self-inductance of a long solenoid, L = μ₀ n² A l with A = πr², giving L = μ₀πn²r²l.
confusing options3 of 4 · options 1, 2, 3
Options 1, 2 and 3 all carry the identical n²r²l dependence and differ only in the constant — 1 is the answer with a spurious factor of 2 (the classic dropped/extra 2 from confusing it with mutual-inductance or energy expressions), 3 is the answer with the area's π forgotten, and only 2 keeps both μ₀ and π correctly; option 4 with μ₀/2π is a Biot–Savart/straight-wire constant and is dismissed on sight.
why it is trickyOnly one recall step and nothing to compute, but the option set is built entirely from constant-factor variants of the same expression, so dimensional check and order-of-magnitude reasoning eliminate nothing — the candidate must remember whether π (from the cross-section) and the factor 2 belong, which is exactly where careless candidates lose it.
the catchDistractors that are the key with a missing π or an extra factor of 2; elimination is useless because all three share the same dimensions and variable dependence.

Q5Physics
subjectPhysics

chapterThermal Properties of Matter high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on volume thermal expansion with gamma = 3 alpha, so dV = 3 alpha (4/3 pi R^3) dT = 4 pi R^3 alpha dT, which is thermal expansion.
subtopic 1Thermal Expansion
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on volume expansion with coefficient 3α, giving ΔV = (4/3)πR^3 · 3αΔT = 4πR^3αΔT, which is taught under thermal expansion.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
The temperature of a metallic sphere of radius R is increased by a small amount Δ T . If the linear coefficient of thermal expansion of the metal is α , the approximate increase in the volume of the sphere is :
  1. 6π R3αΔ T
  2. 2π R3αΔ T
  3. 3π R3αΔ T
  4. 4π R3αΔ T
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (4)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 4 · high
  1. The coefficient of volume expansion is γ = 3α.
  2. The initial volume is V = 43π R3.
  3. Δ V = γ V Δ T = 3α · 43π R3 Δ T.
  4. Δ V = 4π R3αΔ T, which is option 4.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
44 highagree
difficultymedium · 3 steps
stepsRecall volume (cubical) expansion coefficient γ = 3α for an isotropic solid → Write V = 43πR³ for the sphere → Compute ΔV = γVΔT = 3α·43πR³·ΔT and reduce the coefficient
confusing options2 of 4 · options 3, 4
Option 4 is the full product 3α × 43πR³, while option 3 (3πR³αΔT) is exactly what a candidate gets by multiplying 3α by πR³ — i.e. dropping the 4/3 of the sphere volume — and option 1 (6π) and option 2 (2π) correspond to no natural slip.
why it is trickyOnly two or three short steps, but every option has the identical algebraic form and differs only in the numerical coefficient, so no elimination on sight is possible — the candidate must actually carry γ = 3α through the 4/3 πR³ and the commonest careless slip (losing the 4/3) lands squarely on option 3.
the catchPure coefficient-matching distractors: the whole question turns on remembering γ = 3α AND not losing the 4/3 factor, the classic NEET dropped-factor error.

Q6Physics
subjectPhysics
streamnot applicable (Physics has no streams)
branchnot applicable (Physics has no branches)
chapterCurrent Electricity high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on how resistance changes with temperature for positive and negative temperature coefficients (R = R0(1 + alpha*dT)) and how that changes the current in series/parallel combinations, which is taught in Current Electricity.
subtopic 1Temperature Coefficient of Resistance
subtopic 2Combination of Resistors
why subtopic 1The answer depends on how resistances with temperature coefficients +alpha and -alpha change with temperature, R = R0(1 +/- alpha*dT).
why subtopic 2Whether each circuit's current stays constant or changes depends on combining these two resistances in series (sum stays 2R0) versus parallel (equivalent resistance falls), which is the combination-of-resistors result.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Consider two circuits, (A) and (B), each having two resistors. One of them has a positive temperature coefficient of resistance, +α, while the other one has a negative temperature of coefficient, -α, as shown in the figure. The current through these circuits are denoted by IA and IB. At initial temperature, the resistance of the two resistors is R0. As the temperature is increased, the correct option that describes the variation of current in these circuits is :
  1. both IA and IB remain constant
  2. IA remains constant while IB increases
  3. IA decreases while IB increases
  4. IA increases while IB decreases
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (2)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · high
  1. Circuit (A) has the two resistors in series: RA = R0(1-αΔ T) + R0(1+αΔ T) = 2R0.
  2. RA is independent of Δ T, so IA = V2R0 remains constant.
  3. Circuit (B) has them in parallel: RB = R0(1-αΔ T) R0(1+αΔ T)2R0 = R02(1-α2Δ T2).
  4. RB decreases as Δ T increases, so IB = VRB increases, which is option 2.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
22 highagree
difficultyhard · 3 steps
stepsAdd the two series resistances in (A): R0(1-αΔT)+R0(1+αΔT)=2R0, independent of temperature, so I_A stays constant. → Form the parallel combination in (B): product/sum = R0²(1-α²ΔT²)/(2R0) = (R0/2)(1-α²ΔT²). → Notice this is less than R0/2 and shrinks further as ΔT grows (second-order term survives), so I_B increases; match to the option.
confusing options2 of 4 · options 1, 2
Option 1 is exactly what a candidate gets who assumes the ±αΔT corrections cancel in the parallel branch the same way they cancel in series; option 2 requires actually keeping the α²ΔT² term in the product-over-sum.
why it is trickyThree short steps, but the two live options are separated only by a second-order algebraic point that the stem never hints at — the first-order cancellation is identical in both circuits and only the surviving α²ΔT² term in the parallel case breaks the tie, so elimination and intuition both lead straight to the wrong rival.
the catchThe unsignposted insight that (1-x)(1+x)=1-x² makes the parallel resistance fall while the series sum is exactly constant; options 3 and 4 are dead, so the whole question rests on a trap option that is the natural careless answer.

Q7Physics
subjectPhysics
streamnot applicable (Physics has no streams)
branchnot applicable (Physics has no branches)
chapterModern Physics high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on the photoelectric effect: photocurrent is proportional to intensity (power) while the stopping potential depends only on frequency, so it stays constant as power falls.
subtopic 1Photoelectric Effect
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on the photoelectric-effect facts that photocurrent is proportional to light intensity (power) while stopping potential depends only on frequency, so it stays constant.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
A beam of light falls on a metal surface such that photo-electrons are generated. If power of the light source starts to decrease linearly with time t, then variation of the photocurrent I and magnitude of the stopping potential |V| with time is best represented by :
  1. drawn by the pipeline from a figure spec
  2. drawn by the pipeline from a figure spec
  3. drawn by the pipeline from a figure spec
  4. drawn by the pipeline from a figure spec
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (2)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · high
  1. At fixed frequency, the number of photons per second is n = Phν, so the photocurrent I ∝ P.
  2. P(t) = P0 - kt decreases linearly, so I(t) decreases linearly with t.
  3. Stopping potential obeys e|V| = hν - φ, which does not depend on P, so |V| stays constant.
  4. Linearly falling I with constant |V| is the pair of graphs in option 2.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
22 highagree
difficultymedium · 2 steps
stepsLink falling source power to falling intensity, hence photocurrent (number of emitted electrons per second) decreases linearly with t. → Recall that stopping potential depends only on the frequency of the light and the work function, not on intensity, so |V| stays flat; match the pair (falling I, constant |V|) to the correct panel.
confusing options2 of 4 · options 2, 3
Option 3 is exactly what a candidate gets if he wrongly lets stopping potential track intensity — both graphs falling — and it is the single commonest photoelectric misconception; option 2 is the correct split behaviour, and the two differ only in the second panel.
why it is trickyOnly two short recalls, but the options are built as a 2x2 grid of (I constant/falling) x (|V| constant/falling), so elimination gives nothing — the whole question rests on the one fact that stopping potential is intensity-independent, and the rival panel 3 is waiting for anyone who forgets it.
the catchThe graph options encode the classic misconception (stopping potential falling with intensity) as a fully plausible panel; options 1 and 4 are weaker but still have a wrong-route reading, so the candidate cannot shortcut by inspection.

Q8Physics
subjectPhysics
streamnot applicable (Physics has no streams)
branchnot applicable (Physics has no branches)
chapterSemiconductor Electronics high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on the behaviour of a reverse-biased ideal Zener diode at breakdown, which is taught in Semiconductor Electronics.
subtopic 1Zener Diode
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on a reverse-biased Zener diode in breakdown holding 3 V across itself, leaving 5 - 3 = 2 V across the series resistor between B and A.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
An ideal Zener diode with breakdown voltage of -3 V is reverse biased with a negative input voltage Vi = -5 V. The magnitude of voltage difference between points B and A is :
  1. 0 V
  2. 3 V
  3. 2 V
  4. 1 V
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (3)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 3 · high
  1. The Zener (C to B) and resistor R (B to A) are in series across the source of magnitude |Vi| = 5 V.
  2. Since |Vi| = 5 V > |VZ| = 3 V, the reverse-biased ideal Zener is in breakdown, so |VCB| = 3 V.
  3. By KVL, |VBA| = |Vi| - |VZ| = 5 - 3 = 2 V, which is option 3.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
33 highagree
difficultymedium · 2 steps
stepsCheck that |V_i| = 5 V exceeds the 3 V breakdown, so the ideal Zener conducts and clamps C-B at 3 V → Apply KVL: the remaining 5 - 3 = 2 V appears across R, which is exactly the B-to-A segment
confusing options3 of 4 · options 2, 3, 1
Option 2 (3 V) is the Zener's own drop across C-B and is what a candidate grabs who does not check which two points are named; option 1 (0 V) is what you get if you wrongly decide the reverse-biased diode blocks and no current flows through R; option 3 is the key from the 5-3 subtraction. Option 4 (1 V) corresponds to nothing in the circuit.
why it is trickyThe arithmetic is a single subtraction, but the question lives or dies on reading the labels: 3 V and 2 V are both real voltages in this very circuit, one across the diode and one across the resistor, so a careless candidate has a genuinely attractive wrong answer and elimination does not help.
the catchTwo near-identical rivals drawn from the same circuit — the clamped diode drop (C-B) versus the resistor drop (B-A) — plus the prior check that breakdown actually occurs rather than the diode simply blocking.

Q9Physics
subjectPhysics
streamnot applicable (Physics has no streams)
branchnot applicable (Physics has no branches)
chapterMechanical Properties of Fluids high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on the Stokes' law terminal velocity relation v = 2r^2(sigma - rho)g/(9 eta), i.e. v varies linearly with (sigma/rho - 1), which is taught in Mechanical Properties of Fluids.
subtopic 1Stokes' Law and Poiseuille's Equation
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on the Stokes' law terminal velocity relation v = 2r^2(sigma - rho)g/(9 eta), which is linear in (sigma/rho - 1).
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
In the measurement of viscosity of liquids using terminal velocity experiment, spherical balls of same radius but having different densities are used. The variation of the terminal velocity (v) with the ratio of density of spherical ball (σ) to density of the liquid (ρ), is best represented by:
  1. drawn by the pipeline from a figure spec
  2. drawn by the pipeline from a figure spec
  3. drawn by the pipeline from a figure spec
  4. drawn by the pipeline from a figure spec
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (2)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · high
  1. At terminal velocity, weight = buoyancy + viscous drag: 43π r3 σ g = 43π r3 ρ g + 6π η r v.
  2. v = 2 r2 (σ - ρ) g9η.
  3. Rewrite in terms of the ratio: v = 2 r2 ρ g9η(σρ - 1), with r, ρ, η fixed.
  4. So v is a straight line in σ/ρ with positive slope, v = 0 at σ/ρ = 1 (ball only falls for σ/ρ > 1).
  5. The graph starting at σ/ρ = 1 from v = 0 and rising linearly is option 2.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
22 highagree
difficultyhard · 3 steps
stepsRecall Stokes' law terminal velocity: v = (2r²g/9η)(σ − ρ). → Rewrite in terms of the plotted variable: v = (2r²ρg/9η)(σ/ρ − 1), i.e. a straight line in σ/ρ with slope fixed (same radius, same liquid) and x-intercept exactly at σ/ρ = 1. → Note the physical restriction that downward terminal motion (v > 0) exists only for σ/ρ > 1, so the graph must start on the axis at 1 and rise linearly, with nothing to its left.
confusing options3 of 4 · options 2, 3, 4
Options 2, 3 and 4 are all rising straight lines differing only in where they meet the σ/ρ axis — 3 is drawn through the origin (what you get if you wrongly take v ∝ σ and forget the buoyancy term ρ), 4 cuts the axis before 1 (a line with the right shape but the wrong intercept), and only 2 has the intercept exactly at 1; option 1 (constant v, independent of density) is eliminated on sight since the whole formula depends on σ − ρ.
why it is trickyThree steps of work, and the elimination does almost nothing: three of the four graphs are rising lines of the same shape, so the candidate must actually derive the intercept at σ/ρ = 1 from v ∝ (σ − ρ) rather than recognise a picture — the common shortcut 'v grows with density' lands squarely on option 3.
the catchThe answer turns on one quantitative detail of the graph (the x-intercept) that is invisible unless the buoyancy term is kept; dropping ρ from (σ − ρ) gives a through-the-origin line that is offered as a distractor, and the fourth option offers the right shape with a slightly shifted intercept to catch careless reading.

Q10Physics
subjectPhysics
streamnot applicable (Physics has no streams)
branchnot applicable (Physics has no branches)
chapterGravitation high confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on the escape speed formula v = sqrt(2GM/R), so for equal mass v2/v1 = sqrt(R1/R2) = sqrt(2), which is gravitation.
subtopic 1Escape Velocity
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on the escape speed formula v = sqrt(2GM/R), so for equal masses v2/v1 = sqrt(R1/R2) = sqrt(2).
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Two planets P1 and P2 with equal mass have radii R1 and R2, respectively, where R2=R12. The escape speeds of P1 and P2 are v1 and v2, respectively. Then v2v1 is :
  1. 2
  2. 12
  3. 1
  4. 2
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (4)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 4 · high
  1. Escape speed: v = 2GMR.
  2. With equal M: v2v1 = R1R2.
  3. R2 = R12 gives v2v1 = 2, which is option 4.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
44 highagree
difficultymedium · 2 steps
stepsRecall v_esc = 2GM/R, with M equal for both planets so v ∝ 1/√R → Substitute R2 = R1/2 to get v2/v1 = R1/R2 = √2
confusing options3 of 4 · options 1, 2, 4
Option 1 (2) is exactly what a candidate gets by treating v ∝ 1/R and forgetting the square root, and option 2 (1/√2) is the same correct square-root work with the ratio inverted (R2/R1 instead of R1/R2); option 3 is only picked by someone who thinks equal mass means equal escape speed.
why it is trickyOnly one recalled formula and one substitution, but the three numerical rivals are precisely the two commonest slips — dropping the square root and flipping the radius ratio — so elimination by inspection does not work and the candidate must actually write v ∝ 1/√R and keep the direction straight.
the catchThe distractors are the exact outputs of the forgotten-square-root and inverted-ratio errors, both of which are fast, tempting routes under time pressure.

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · high

why this chapterThe answer depends on the self-induced emf E = L dI/dt across a single inductor (P) versus two identical inductors in parallel with equivalent inductance L/2 (Q), giving a ratio of 2, which is self-inductance in electromagnetic induction.
subtopic 1Coefficient of Self-Inductance of Two Coils in Series and Parallel
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on the parallel combination of the two inductors giving L/2 across a-b in Q versus a single L across a-b in P, so E_P/E_Q = L/(L/2) = 2.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Two identical inductors are connected in two different configurations P and Q, where a time varying current I(t) is flowing, as shown in the figure. The induced emf between points a and b for configuration P is EP and that for configuration Q is EQ. The ratio EP / EQ is : [Neglect the effect of mutual inductance.]
  1. 2
  2. 1/4
  3. 1/2
  4. 1
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (1)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 1 · high
  1. From the figure, in P the points a and b lie across only the upper inductor, which carries the full current I(t).
  2. EP = LdIdt.
  3. In Q, a and b lie across the two identical inductors in parallel, so Leq = L · LL+L = L2, carrying total current I(t).
  4. EQ = L2dIdt.
  5. EPEQ = LL/2 = 2, which is option 1.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
11 highagree
difficultyhard · 3 steps
stepsRead configuration P carefully: point a is above the first coil and b is below it, so only ONE inductor lies between a and b — the second coil sits below b, outside the a–b span, giving E_P = L dI/dt. → Read configuration Q: the two identical coils are in parallel between a and b, so the equivalent inductance is L/2 and E_Q = (L/2) dI/dt (equivalently each branch carries I/2). → Form the ratio E_P/E_Q.
confusing options3 of 4 · options 1, 2, 3
Option 1 is the result of correctly seeing one coil in P against a parallel pair in Q; option 3 is the same magnitude with the ratio inverted (Q over P), the commonest slip; option 2 is where a candidate lands who treats P as two coils in series (ratio 4), fails to find 4 among the choices, and then flips it to 1/4.
why it is trickyOnly three short lines of algebra, but the whole question turns on an unsignposted figure reading — that a and b bracket just one of the two coils in P — and three of the four options are reachable by plausible wrong routes (series reading, inverted ratio), so elimination gives nothing and the figure must be parsed exactly.
the catchThe trap is geometric, not computational: almost every candidate reads P as 'two in series' and Q as 'two in parallel' and gets 4, which is deliberately absent, pushing them toward the inverted 1/4 rather than back to a re-read of where b sits.

subtopic 1Energy Stored in a Capacitor

why subtopic 1The answer is a ratio of stored energies, U = (1/2)CV^2 for capacitor P versus the whole system, which is taught under energy stored in a capacitor.
why subtopic 2Getting the voltage across P (V/2) and the equivalent capacitance 3C/2 requires solving the mixed series-parallel network (P and Q in series, parallel with S), which is the combination-of-capacitors skill.

READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Three identical capacitors P, Q and S, each of the capacitance C, are connected to a battery of voltage V, as shown in the figure. If the energy stored in the capacitor P and total energy stored in the system are Up and Ut, respectively, then the ratio Up / Ut is :
  1. 1/6
  2. 2/3
  3. 1/3
  4. 1/2
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (1)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 1 · high
  1. From the figure, P and Q are in series, and that branch is in parallel with S across the battery V.
  2. Identical capacitors in series share the voltage equally, so VP = VQ = V2.
  3. Up = 12 C (V2)2 = CV28.
  4. Energy in the P-Q branch: UPQ = 12·C2 V2 = CV24.
  5. Energy in S: US = 12 C V2.
  6. Ut = CV24 + CV22 = 3CV24.
  7. UpUt = CV2/83CV2/4 = 16, which is option 1.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
11 highagree
difficultyhard · 5 steps
stepsRead the figure: P and Q form a series branch across the battery, and S is a separate branch directly across the battery. → Split the voltage on the series branch: each of P and Q carries V/2. → Energy in P = ½C(V/2)² = CV²/8. → Energy of the whole system = series pair ½(C/2)V² = CV²/4 plus S's ½CV² = 3CV²/4. → Form the ratio CV²/8 ÷ 3CV²/4.
confusing options3 of 4 · options 1, 3, 4
Option 3 13 is exactly what a candidate gets by forgetting the voltage split and crediting P with the whole series-branch energy CV²/4 over the total 3CV²/4; option 4 12 is the ratio obtained if S is left out of the total or if the series pair is mistaken for a parallel pair; only the correct half-voltage treatment of P lands on 1/6.
why it is trickyFour to five short steps, and the distractors are not decoration — the single commonest slip (treating P as carrying the full V, or as carrying the series combination's whole energy) lands exactly on 1/3, and dropping S from the total lands on 1/2, so elimination gives nothing and the circuit reading plus the energy sum must both be done correctly.
the catchThe whole question turns on noticing that P only sees V/2 while S sees the full V; two of the three wrong options are precisely the results of the two standard shortcuts, so a careless but prepared candidate has a plausible-looking answer waiting.

chapterElectromagnetic Induction high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on the EMF induced by the changing flux through the loop (Faraday's law, e = -B dA/dt), whose square over R gives the dissipated power P.
subtopic 1Faraday's Law of Electromagnetic Induction
subtopic 2Calculate Thermal Energy Developed
why subtopic 1The answer depends on the induced emf e = -dPhi/dt = -B A0 cos t from the time-varying area via Faraday's law, giving P = e^2/R proportional to cos^2 t.
why subtopic 2The quantity asked for is the Joule power dissipated in the resistive loop (P = e^2/R), which is the heat-developed calculation this topic covers.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
A conducting loop of finite resistance lies on the x - y plane. There is a constant magnetic field in the z direction. The area of the loop varies with time t, as A = A0(1 + sin t) in appropriate units. The figure that correctly indicates the qualitative behaviour of the power P dissipated in the loop as a function of time is :
  1. drawn by the pipeline on the booklet's own geometry, its figure spec checked against it (labels and lines)
  2. drawn by the pipeline from a figure spec
  3. drawn by the pipeline from a figure spec
  4. drawn by the pipeline on the booklet's own geometry, its figure spec checked against it (labels and lines)
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (3)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 3 · high
  1. Flux through the loop: φ = B A = B A0 (1 + sin t).
  2. Induced emf: ε = -dφdt = -B A0 cos t.
  3. Power dissipated: P = ε2R = B2 A02R cos2 t.
  4. At t = 0, P = B2 A02R (maximum, non-zero); at t = π2, P = 0; then P rises again, and P ≥ 0 always.
  5. Option 1 (monotonic rise from 0) and option 4 (constant) are wrong; option 2 starts at P = 0 (that is sin2 t); option 3 starts at a non-zero maximum and dips to zero, so the answer is option 3.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
33 highagree
difficultyhard · 4 steps
stepsWrite flux Φ = B·A = B·A₀(1 + sin t) with B constant → Differentiate: emf = -dΦ/dt = -B A₀ cos t → Power P = emf²/R = (B²A₀²/R) cos² t — periodic, non-negative, zero twice per cycle → Match to the graph: P must START AT ITS MAXIMUM at t = 0 and dip to zero, not start at zero
confusing options2 of 4 · options 2, 3
Options 2 and 3 are both non-negative periodic humps touching zero, and they differ only in phase — 2 is the sin²-type curve starting at zero (what you draw if you carry the sin t of the area straight into the power and forget that differentiation turns it into cos), while 3 starts at the maximum as cos²t actually does; options 1 (monotonic exponential rise) and 4 (constant) contradict the periodic driving on sight.
why it is trickyFour short steps — flux, differentiate, square, read the graph — and crucially two genuinely live option shapes that only the phase of the derivative separates, so elimination gets you to 2 vs 3 but no further without doing the differentiation correctly.
the catchThe whole question turns on one easily lost detail: d/dt(sin t) = cos t, so the power peaks at t = 0 rather than vanishing there; a candidate who skips that lands exactly on the sin²-shaped distractor, which looks like the 'obvious' answer because it echoes the sin t printed in the stem.

In an adiabatic expansion, the temperature of one mole of an ideal monatomic gas (γ = 5/3) decreases from 60 K to 50 K. The work done by the gas in the process is : (Take the universal gas constant as R = 8.3 J mol-1 K-1)

  2. 41.5 J
  3. 83 J

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 4 · high
  1. For an adiabatic process the work done by the gas is W = nR(T1 - T2)γ - 1.
  2. γ - 1 = 53 - 1 = 23.
  3. nR(T1 - T2) = 1 × 8.3 × (60 - 50) = 83 J.

stepsRecognise adiabatic: Q = 0, so W = -ΔU = nC_v(T1 - T2) → Get C_v from γ = 5/3: C_v = R/(γ-1) = 3R/2 → Compute 1 × 32(8.3)(10) = 124.5 J

chapterKinematics high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on computing total distance travelled (accounting for the velocity reversal at t = 3 s) divided by time to get average speed, which is one-dimensional kinematics.
subtopic 1Average Speed
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer is total distance (18 m, accounting for the reversal at t = 3 s) divided by total time (6 s), i.e. the definition of average speed.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Consider a particle moving along a straight line, whose position as a function of time is given by s(t) = α t2 - β t + γ, where α = 1 m s-2, β = 6 m s-1 and γ = 5 m. The average speed of the particle, in m s-1, from t = 0 to t = 6 s is:
  1. 0
  2. 12
  3. 6
  4. 3
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (4)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 4 · high
  1. With the given constants, s(t) = t2 - 6t + 5 (in m).
  2. v = dsdt = 2t - 6, which is zero at t = 3 s, so the particle reverses direction there.
  3. s(0) = 5 m, s(3) = 9 - 18 + 5 = -4 m, s(6) = 36 - 36 + 5 = 5 m.
  4. Distance = |s(3) - s(0)| + |s(6) - s(3)| = 9 + 9 = 18 m.
  5. Average speed = 18 m6 s = 3 m s-1, which is option 4.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
44 highagree
difficultyhard · 4 steps
stepsWrite s(t) = t² − 6t + 5 and differentiate to get v = 2t − 6 → Find the turning point v = 0 at t = 3 s, recognising the particle reverses direction inside the interval → Evaluate s(0) = 5 m, s(3) = −4 m, s(6) = 5 m → Add the two path lengths |−4−5| + |5−(−4)| = 18 m and divide by 6 s
confusing options3 of 4 · options 1, 3, 4
Option 1 is exactly what a candidate gets by using displacement (s(6) = s(0), so average velocity is zero) instead of distance; option 3 is the instantaneous speed at t = 6 s (and also half of 36/6 style slips); option 4 is the key from the full distance 18 m over 6 s.
why it is trickyFour steps and at least two genuine rivals: the whole question hinges on noticing the sign change of v at t = 3 s, and a candidate who skips that check lands cleanly on option 1 by computing average velocity, which is the single commonest error on this item type.
the catchThe unsignposted insight is that the particle turns around inside the interval, so speed ≠ |velocity|; the distractor '0' is precisely the displacement-based answer, and the arithmetic (three position evaluations plus a sum of magnitudes) is longer to execute than it looks.

chapterThermodynamics high confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on the law of equipartition of energy giving Cv and Cp/Cv of a polyatomic gas from its degrees of freedom (each vibrational mode contributing 2 halves of R), which this taxonomy files under Thermodynamics (topic: Specific Heat Capacity of Monoatomic, Diatomic, and Polyatomic Gases).
subtopic 1Specific Heat Capacity of Monoatomic, Diatomic, and Polyatomic Gases
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on relating Cp/Cv of a polyatomic gas to its translational, rotational and vibrational degrees of freedom (gamma = 1 + 2/f_total), which is taught under specific heat capacities of polyatomic gases.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
An ideal gas is made of polyatomic molecules. Each of the molecules has three translational, three rotational and f number of vibrational modes. If the ratio of heat capacities CpCv of the gas is 87, then the value of f is:
  1. 1
  2. 4
  3. 3
  4. 2
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (2)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · high
  1. Each translational and rotational mode contributes 1 degree of freedom; each vibrational mode contributes 2 (kinetic and potential), so F = 3 + 3 + 2f = 6 + 2f.
  2. For an ideal gas γ = CpCv = 1 + 2F.
  3. 1 + 2F = 87 ⇒ 2F = 17 ⇒ F = 14.
  4. 6 + 2f = 14 ⇒ f = 4, which is option 2.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
22 highagree
difficultymedium · 3 steps
stepsFrom γ = Cp/Cv = 8/7, get Cv = R/(γ−1) = 7R, i.e. total degrees of freedom N with Cv = (N/2)R gives N = 14 → Write N in terms of modes, remembering each vibrational mode contributes 2 (kinetic + potential): N = 3 + 3 + 2f → Solve 6 + 2f = 14 for f
confusing options2 of 4 · options 2, 1
Option 2 is the correct f from counting each vibrational mode twice, while a candidate who sets Cv = 72R instead of 7R (mis-handling γ−1 = 1/7) lands on a small f and picks 1; options 3 and 4 are small-number noise with no clean wrong route.
why it is trickyShort to execute but three linked steps — invert γ to Cv, convert Cv to degrees of freedom, then apply the vibrational doubling — and the doubling is the whole question: it is unsignposted and a candidate who omits it gets 8, which is absent from the list, so the careless route feeds back into the arithmetic-slip option 1 rather than self-correcting.
the catchThe factor of 2 for each vibrational mode (kinetic + potential) is the hidden rule; everything else is routine formula work.

Q19Physics
subjectPhysics
streamnot applicable (Physics has no streams)
branchnot applicable (Physics has no branches)
chapterElectrostatics high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on the work done as the potential difference between two points outside a uniformly charged sphere (path-independent, conservative electrostatic field), which gives n = 18.
subtopic 1Work Done by Electric Force
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer is the work done in moving a unit charge between two points outside the charged sphere, W = q(V_A - V_B) = (rho R^2/3epsilon_0)(1/2 - 1/3) = rho R^2/(18 epsilon_0), which is independent of the path through the tube.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
A unit positive point charge is taken slowly through an infinitesimally thin tube that is inside a charged dielectric sphere of radius R, having uniform positive charge density ρ, as shown in the figure. The initial and final positions of the charge are marked by A and B at distances 2R and 3R respectively, from the centre of the sphere. In this process, the magnitude of the total work done on the point charge is ρ R2nε0. The value of n is : ε0 is the permittivity of vacuum)
  1. 18
  2. 2
  3. 6
  4. 9
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (1)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 1 · high
  1. The field is conservative, so the work depends only on the end points: W = q(VB - VA) with q = 1.
  2. Total charge of the sphere: Q = 43π R3 ρ.
  3. Outside the sphere (r ≥ R): V(r) = Q4πε0 r = ρ R33ε0 r.
  4. At A (r = 2R): VA = ρ R26ε0.
  5. At B (r = 3R): VB = ρ R29ε0.
  6. |W| = |VB - VA| = ρ R2ε0(16 - 19) = ρ R218ε0.
  7. So n = 18, which is option 1.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
11 highagree
difficultyhard · 4 steps
stepsNotice both A (2R) and B (3R) lie OUTSIDE the sphere of radius R, so the tube through the interior is irrelevant — the electrostatic force is conservative and only the endpoint potentials matter → Write total charge Q = ρ·43πR³ → Use external potential V = Q/(4πε₀r) and form V_A − V_B = Q/(4πε₀)·(1/2R − 1/3R) = Q/(24πε₀R) → Substitute Q and simplify to ρR²/(nε₀), reading off n
confusing options3 of 4 · options 1, 3, 4
Option 3 (6) is exactly what you get if the 1/3 of the sphere-volume formula is dropped (Q = 4πR³ρ), option 4 (9) comes from mishandling the 1/2R − 1/3R difference (e.g. using 1/(3R) doubled or halving the 1/6R), and only the clean algebra lands on 18; option 2 is an order of magnitude off and dies on sight.
why it is trickyHard: the figure is built to make the candidate start integrating the interior field through the tube when in fact both endpoints are outside the sphere and the whole thing collapses to a two-point potential difference — that unsignposted insight, plus a four-factor algebraic chain (volume charge, 1/4πε₀, 1/6R) in which the two most common slips land exactly on options 6 and 9, puts it above a routine potential problem.
the catchThe deliberately misleading tube/figure hides the path-independence shortcut, and the distractors are precisely the results of dropping the 1/3 in the sphere volume or botching 1/2R − 1/3R, so elimination gives no help.

chapterMagnetic Effect of Electric Current high confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on adding the Biot-Savart fields of the two arc segments (carrying currents split in inverse ratio of their resistances) at the centre of the loop, which is the magnetic effect of electric current.
subtopic 1Magnetic Field Due to a Current Element, Biot–Savart Law
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on the Biot-Savart result for the field of a semicircular arc at its centre (mu0 i/4r), applied to the unequal currents 2I0/3 and I0/3 in the two halves to give mu0 I0/12r.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
A current I0 flows through a metallic circular loop of radius r as shown in the figure. Resistance of the segment ABC is half of ADC. Magnitude of magnetic field at the center O of the loop is:
  1. μ0 I02π r
  2. μ0 I012r
  3. μ0 I04r
  4. μ0 I02r
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (2)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · high
  1. A and C are diametrically opposite, so ABC and ADC are semicircles in parallel.
  2. Since RABC = 12RADC, current divides inversely: IABC = 2I03, IADC = I03.
  3. Field of a semicircular arc at its centre: B = μ0 i4r.
  4. The two arcs carry current in opposite senses about O, so their fields oppose: B = μ04r(2I03 - I03).
  5. The straight leads lie along the line through O, so they contribute 0.
  6. B = μ0 I012r, which is option 2.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
22 highagree
difficultyhard · 4 steps
stepsRecognise ABC and ADC are parallel branches; with R(ABC) = R(ADC)/2 the currents split inversely as 2:1, so I_ABC = 2I0/3 and I_ADC = I0/3. → Field at centre of a semicircular arc of radius r carrying current I is μ0I/(4r). → Note the two branch currents circulate in opposite senses around O, so their fields are antiparallel and must be subtracted. → Net B = μ0/(4r)·(2I0/3 − I0/3) = μ0I0/(12r).
confusing options3 of 4 · options 2, 3, 4
Option 3 (μ0I0/4r) is exactly what you get if you take one semicircle carrying the full I0 and forget the split or the cancellation; option 4 (μ0I0/2r) is the full-loop formula, reached by anyone who treats the loop as a single circular turn carrying I0; only option 1 with its 2πr (straight-wire form) is dead on sight.
why it is trickyFour short steps — current division by resistance, arc-field formula, opposite senses, subtraction — and two genuine rivals sitting on the two commonest shortcuts (ignore the split, or treat it as a whole loop), so elimination does not rescue a careless candidate.
the catchThe familiar version of this problem has equal halves and gives zero; here the altered resistance ratio means the memorised 'fields cancel' response has no matching option, and the sign/direction of the two arc contributions is the step most often lost.

subtopic 1Problems on Elastic Collision

Bob B of mass m at rest is hanging vertically from the ceiling via a massless string of length 10 m, as shown in the figure. Point mass A of mass m travelling horizontally with speed 10 ms-1 hits bob B elastically. The bob B rises h meter after the collision. Taking the acceleration due to gravity g = 10 ms-2 and neglecting the size of the bob, the value of h is :
  1. 2.5
  2. 8
  3. 7
  4. 5
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (4)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 4 · high
  1. In a head-on elastic collision between equal masses with B at rest, velocities are exchanged, so vB = 10 m s-1.
  2. Check whether B rises above the horizontal: vB2 = 100 < 2gL = 2 × 10 × 10 = 200, so B stops before the string becomes horizontal and the string stays taut.
  3. Energy conservation: 12 m vB2 = m g h.
  4. h = vB22g = 10020 = 5 m, which is option 4.

Option 4 is the clean exchange-of-velocity plus energy answer, while option 1 (2.5) is the trap for a candidate who halves the speed after collision (treating it as a shared-velocity/inelastic hit) or halves the rise; options 2 and 3 (8 and 7) correspond to no standard wrong route and are discarded on sight.

printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
An electromagnetic wave travelling in a lossless dielectric medium having a dielectric constant, εr = 9, has the electric field, Ex = E0 sin(kz - 2π × 106 t) V m-1 where E0 is the amplitude and k is the wave vector. Among the following options, the incorrect choice is:
  1. The direction of propagation of the electromagnetic wave is along +z
  2. The speed of the electromagnetic wave inside the medium is 108 m s-1
  3. The wavelength of the electromagnetic wave inside the medium is 300 m
  4. The magnetic field is given by the relation By = B0ν sin(kz - 2π × 106 t) where ν is the speed of the electromagnetic wave inside the medium
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (3 & 4)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 3 · medium
  1. The phase kz - ω t means the wave travels along +z, so option 1 is correct.
  2. With μr = 1, v = cεr = 3× 1089 = 108 m s-1, so option 2 is correct.
  3. ω = 2π × 106 rad s-1 gives f = ω2π = 106 Hz.
  4. λ = vf = 108106 = 100 m ≠ 300 m, so option 3 is incorrect.
  5. For E along x and propagation along +z, B lies along y with By = E0vsin(kz - ω t), which option 4 intends.
  6. The incorrect choice is option 3.

difficultyhard · 5 steps
stepsRead the phase (kz - ωt) and conclude propagation is along +z, so option 1 is correct and not the answer. → Compute refractive index n = ε_r = 3 and medium speed v = c/n = 1×108 m/s, confirming option 2. → Extract frequency from ω = 2π×106 giving f = 106 Hz. → Compute λ = v/f = 108/106 = 100 m and compare with the stated 300 m. → Check the B-field relation: amplitude should be E0/v, so the printed B0/v must also be scrutinised.

chapterWork, Energy and Power high confidence
why this chapterThe answer comes from integrating a position-dependent friction force over displacement (work done by a variable force), giving magnitude mu0*M*g*L/2.
subtopic 1Work Done by Variable Force
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer comes from integrating a position-dependent friction force, mu_0(1 - x/L)Mg, over 0 to L, giving a work of magnitude (1/2)mu_0 MgL, which is a variable-force work calculation.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
A particle of mass M moves along a horizontal x axis from x = 0 to x = L. The coefficient of kinetic friction varies as a function of x as μk(x) = μ0 - α x, where μ0, α are constants of appropriate dimensions, so that μk(L) = 0. The total work done by the frictional force during the motion is nμ0 MgL, where g is the acceleration due to gravity. The value of n is:
  1. 12
  2. 3
  3. 1
  4. 13
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (1)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 1 · high
  1. From μk(L) = 0: μ0 - α L = 0 ⇒ α = μ0L.
  2. Friction force magnitude at x: f(x) = μk(x) M g = (μ0 - α x) M g.
  3. |W| = ∫0L (μ0 - α x) M g dx = M g(μ0 L - α L22).
  4. Substitute α = μ0L: |W| = M g(μ0 L - μ0 L2) = 12μ0 M g L.
  5. So n = 12 (in magnitude), which is option 1.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
11 highagree
difficultymedium · 3 steps
stepsUse the condition μ_k(L) = 0 to fix α = μ₀/L → Write the elementary friction work dW = -μ_k(x)Mg dx and integrate from 0 to L → Evaluate μ₀L - αL²/2 = μ₀L/2 and read off n (magnitude 1/2)
confusing options2 of 4 · options 1, 3
Option 3 (n = 1) is exactly what a candidate gets by treating friction as constant μ₀ over the whole length and never doing the integral, while 1/2 comes from the linear average; option 4 13 only arises from mistakenly integrating an x² law and option 2 (3) has no plausible route at all.
why it is trickyMedium: three short steps (fix α from the boundary condition, integrate a linear μ, evaluate) and the integral is trivial, but the constant-μ shortcut lands squarely on the live rival n = 1, so the candidate must actually notice that μ falls linearly to zero.
the catchThe whole question turns on the hidden step α = μ₀/L buried in 'μ_k(L) = 0'; skipping it or averaging carelessly gives the printed distractor 1.

  2. 0

  1. O is at the centre of Q, so its real distance from each interface is d = 52 = 2.5 cm.

subtopic 1Electrostatic Potential Energy
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer comes from energy conservation: the loss in electrostatic potential energy q[kQ/R - kQ/3R] = 2kQq/(3R) of the point charge-sphere system becomes kinetic energy, giving v = sqrt(Qq/(3*pi*epsilon0*m*R)).
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Consider a fixed uniformly charged insulating sphere with radius R and total charge +Q. A point charge -q (q << Q) with mass m is released from rest at a distance of 3R from the centre of the charged sphere. When the point charge reaches the surface of the sphere, its speed is : (ε0 is the permittivity of vacuum, neglect gravitational forces).
  1. Qq4πε0 mR
  2. 3Qq4πε0 mR
  3. 2Qq3πε0 mR
  4. Qq3πε0 mR
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (4)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 4 · high
  1. Outside the sphere the potential is V(r) = Q4πε0 r, so the charge -q has potential energy U(r) = -Qq4πε0 r.
  2. Energy conservation from r=3R (at rest) to r=R: 12mv2 = U(3R) - U(R) = Qq4πε0(1R - 13R).
  3. 12mv2 = Qq4πε0·23R = Qq6πε0 R.
  4. v2 = Qq3πε0 mR, so v = Qq3πε0 mR, which is option 4.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict

stepsRecognise that both the start point (3R) and the end point (surface, r = R) are at or outside the sphere, so the external potential V = Q/(4πε₀r) applies at both — no internal-field integral is needed → Write energy conservation for the attracting charge: ½mv² = kQq(1/R − 1/3R) = 2kQq/(3R) → Solve for v and fold k = 1/(4πε₀) into the constant, simplifying 4/(3·4π) to 1/(3π)
confusing options3 of 4 · options 1, 3, 4

why it is trickyThree steps and no elimination available — every option has the right dimensions and the same symbols, so the candidate must actually carry the (1/R − 1/3R) difference and the ½ through the algebra; two of the distractors sit exactly on the two commonest slips, which is what lifts this above a one-look energy-conservation plug-in.
the catchThe distractors are built from specific arithmetic slips (forgetting the initial-potential term; dropping the factor of 2 from ½mv²) and the final constant must be simplified from 4/(3·4πε₀) to 1/(3πε₀), a step where the answer can look 'wrong' against the familiar 4πε₀ forms in options 1 and 2.

Q27Physics

chapterCenter of Mass medium confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on the total linear momentum of a two-particle system as the vector sum of the particles' momenta (equivalently M times the centre-of-mass velocity), giving P = 2mv|cos theta|, which rises from zero at A to a maximum and falls back to zero at B.
subtopic 1Problems on Velocity, Acceleration and Force of Center of Mass
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on the total linear momentum of the two-particle system, P = (sum of masses) x velocity of the centre of mass, and how it varies with theta as the particles move.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
A frictionless circular wire of unit radius is fixed on the horizontal plane. Two point particles of unit mass start moving simultaneously from point A(θ=π2) with identical uniform angular speeds in opposite directions, and meet again at point B(θ=-π2). During this time, which of the following figures schematically represent the magnitude of the total linear momentum P of the system, as a function of θ ?
  1. drawn by the pipeline from a figure spec
  2. drawn by the pipeline from a figure spec
  3. drawn by the pipeline from a figure spec
  4. drawn by the pipeline from a figure spec
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (4)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 4 · high
  1. The particles stay symmetric about the vertical diameter: one at angle θ, the other at π-θ.
  2. Velocity of the clockwise particle at θ: →v1 = ω(sinθ, -cosθ).
  3. Velocity of the anticlockwise particle at π-θ: →v2 = ω(-sinθ, -cosθ).
  4. Total momentum →P = m(→v1 + →v2) = (0, -2mωcosθ), so |P| = 2mω|cosθ|.
  5. As θ goes from π2 to -π2, |P| rises from 0 to its maximum 2mω at θ = 0 and falls back to 0, an arch shape, which is option 4.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
44 highagree
difficultyhard · 4 steps
stepsRecognise the symmetry: if one particle is at angle θ, the other (started together at π/2, opposite sense) is at π − θ, mirrored about the vertical axis. → Write each tangential velocity as a vector: ω(sin θ, −cos θ) for the clockwise particle and ω(−sin θ, −cos θ) for the counter-clockwise one. → Add them — the horizontal components cancel, leaving P = 2ω|cos θ| directed along the vertical. → Match the function 2ω|cos θ| over θ from π/2 down to −π/2 to the sketched curves: zero at both ends, maximum at θ = 0, i.e. a single arch.
confusing options3 of 4 · options 2, 3, 4
Option 2 (constant P) is exactly what a candidate who says 'no external force, momentum conserved' writes down, forgetting the wire's normal reactions; option 3 is the inverted arch a candidate gets if they attach the zero to θ = 0 instead of to the endpoints (or compute a |sin θ| dependence); option 4 is the correct 2ω|cos θ| arch. Only option 1 (a straight line) corresponds to no sensible calculation and dies on sight.
why it is trickyHard: it takes four genuine steps — exploit the mirror symmetry to locate the second particle, write two tangential velocity vectors with correct signs, add and take the magnitude, then read the functional shape off a graph — and after all that three of the four sketches are still live, with the 'momentum is conserved so P is constant' trap (option 2) catching exactly the candidate who does no vector work at all.
the catchThe unstated point is that the wire exerts normal forces, so total linear momentum is NOT conserved; a sign error in one tangential velocity flips the cancellation from the horizontal to the vertical component and lands precisely on the inverted-arch distractor (option 3). The answer is a shape rather than a number, so there is no numerical sanity check to catch the error.

Q28Physics
subjectPhysics
streamnot applicable (Physics has no streams)
branchnot applicable (Physics has no branches)
chapterSemiconductor Electronics high confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on how the depletion-region width of a p-n junction diode changes under forward versus reverse bias, which is semiconductor electronics.
subtopic 1PN Junction Diode Under Reverse Bias
subtopic 2PN Junction Diode Under Forward Bias
why subtopic 1The answer W3 > W2 > W1 rests on the depletion region widening under reverse bias (D3), so D3's is the widest, compared with the unbiased D2 in the open branch.
why subtopic 2The answer also needs the depletion region to narrow under forward bias (D1), so D1's width is the smallest; the question directly compares the two bias conditions.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Three identical p-n junction diodes D1, D2 and D3 are connected across a battery as shown in the figure. If the width of the depletion regions of D1, D2 and D3 are W1, W2 and W3, respectively, then the correct option is:
  1. W2 > W1 = W3
  2. W1 > W2 > W3
  3. W3 = W1 > W2
  4. W3 > W2 > W1
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (4)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 4 · medium
  1. The battery's long plate (positive) is on the left, so the left junction is at higher potential than the right junction.
  2. D1 points from left to right, so its anode is at the higher potential: forward biased, so W1 is less than the unbiased width.
  3. D3 points from right to left, so its cathode is at the higher potential: reverse biased, so W3 is greater than the unbiased width.
  4. The branch with D2 is broken (open gap), so no voltage acts on D2: it is unbiased and W2 keeps its equilibrium value.
  5. Therefore W3 > W2 > W1, which is option 4.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
44 mediumagree
difficultyhard · 3 steps
stepsRead the circuit: three parallel branches across the 5 V battery, and notice the small gap (two dots not joined) in the middle branch containing D2 — that branch is open. → Fix the bias of each diode from the arrow direction relative to the battery terminals: D1 is forward biased, D3 is reverse biased, and D2 carries no current and sits at zero applied bias (equilibrium width). → Map bias to depletion width: forward bias narrows it, zero bias leaves it at the equilibrium value, reverse bias widens it, then order W1, W2, W3.
confusing options3 of 4 · options 1, 2, 4
Option 4 and option 2 are exact mirror images of each other, so a candidate who inverts the forward/reverse–width rule lands precisely on 2; option 1 is what you get if you miss the break and treat D2 as the odd one out while assuming the other two behave alike; option 3 (W3 = W1) requires both an equality that no reading of the figure supports and is the weakest of the four.
why it is trickyThree steps, and the options do not narrow anything — the key and its exact reversal are both on the paper, so elimination is useless and the whole question rests on two unsignposted judgements: spotting the tiny break in the middle branch and remembering which way bias moves the depletion width.
the catchThe open middle branch is a drawing detail, not stated in words; a candidate who misses it gives D2 a forward bias and the answer collapses. On top of that, the forward-narrows/reverse-widens rule is exactly the kind of fact students invert, and option 2 is built to catch that inversion.

Q29Physics
subjectPhysics
streamnot applicable (Physics has no streams)
branchnot applicable (Physics has no branches)
chapterRay Optics high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on applying the thin lens formula successively to two separated lenses (convex then concave), which is ray optics.
subtopic 1Lens Formula
subtopic 2Position of Equivalent Lens When Two Lenses are Separated by a Distance
why subtopic 1The answer comes from applying the lens formula twice: L1 (f=+10 cm, u=-30 cm) forms an image at +15 cm, which is a virtual object 12 cm beyond L2 (f=-10 cm), giving v=-60 cm, i.e. 60 cm to the left of the concave lens.
why subtopic 2The setup is a two-lens system with the lenses separated by 3 cm, which is the situation this topic covers, so a student revising separated lens combinations should see it.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
The lens combination as shown in the figure, consists of two lenses, L1 and L2, of the focal lengths + 10 cm and - 10 cm, respectively. The position of the image formed is :
  1. 60 cm to the right of the concave lens
  2. 20 cm to the left of the concave lens
  3. 60 cm to the left of the concave lens
  4. 30 cm to the right of the concave lens
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (3)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 3 · high
  1. For L1: u1 = -30 cm, f1 = +10 cm.
  2. 1v1 = 1f1 + 1u1 = 110 - 130 = 115, so v1 = +15 cm right of L1.
  3. L2 is 3 cm right of L1, so the image acts as a virtual object at u2 = 15 - 3 = +12 cm.
  4. 1v2 = 1f2 + 1u2 = -110 + 112 = -160.
  5. v2 = -60 cm, i.e. 60 cm to the left of the concave lens, which is option 3.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
33 highagree
difficultyhard · 4 steps
stepsApply the lens formula to L1 with u = -30 cm, f = +10 cm to get the first image at 15 cm to the right of L1. → Shift the reference: this image lies 15 - 3 = 12 cm to the right of L2, so it acts as a VIRTUAL object for the concave lens (u = +12 cm). → Apply the lens formula to L2 with u = +12 cm, f = -10 cm: 1/v = -1/10 + 1/12 = -1/60. → Interpret the negative v as a position on the object side, i.e. 60 cm to the left of the concave lens.
confusing options3 of 4 · options 1, 3, 4
Option 3 and option 1 are the same magnitude with opposite sides, so anyone who mishandles the sign of the virtual object (u = +12) or misreads a negative v lands on 1; option 4 is exactly what you get if you forget to subtract the 3 cm separation and feed u = +15 into the second lens, which gives 30 cm.
why it is trickyThree or four short calculations, but the options are built directly on the two classic slips in this problem — forgetting the 3 cm gap (gives the 30 cm pair) and mishandling the sign of a virtual object (gives right instead of left) — so elimination by magnitude or by side both fail and the candidate must carry the signs correctly through both lens equations.
the catchThe virtual-object step: the intermediate image lies beyond the second lens, so u is positive, a case most students meet rarely and whose sign error lands exactly on a printed option.

Q30Physics
subjectPhysics
streamnot applicable (Physics has no streams)
branchnot applicable (Physics has no branches)
chapterRotational Motion high confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on applying the parallel axis theorem to the moments of inertia of two solid spheres about axes through each centre, which is rotational motion.
subtopic 1Parallel Axis and Perpendicular Axis Theorem
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer (m - M)(R + r)^2 comes from shifting each sphere's moment of inertia to the other sphere's centre with the parallel axis theorem, where the 2/5 MR^2 and 2/5 mr^2 terms cancel in the difference.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
A solid sphere A of radius R and mass M is attached at a point to a smaller solid sphere B of radius r < R and mass m < M. Assume that the line joining their centres lies along the horizontal. The moment of inertia of the system calculated about a vertical axis passing through the centre of A is IA and that calculated about a vertical axis passing through the centre of B is IB. The difference IA - IB is:
  1. 0
  2. (M - m)(R + r)2
  3. (m - M)(R + r)2
  4. (m - M)(R - r)2
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (3)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 3 · high
  1. The spheres touch, so the distance between their centres is d = R + r.
  2. About the axis through A: IA = 25MR2 + 25mr2 + m(R+r)2 (parallel-axis theorem for B).
  3. About the axis through B: IB = 25MR2 + 25mr2 + M(R+r)2 (parallel-axis theorem for A).
  4. IA - IB = m(R+r)2 - M(R+r)2 = (m - M)(R+r)2, which is option 3.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
33 highagree
difficultymedium · 3 steps
stepsRecognise the spheres touch, so the centre-to-centre separation is R + r. → Write both moments by the parallel-axis theorem: I_A = 25MR² + 25mr² + m(R+r)² (B shifted) and I_B = 25MR² + M(R+r)² + 25mr² (A shifted). → Subtract: the two self-inertia terms cancel, leaving (m − M)(R + r)².
confusing options2 of 4 · options 2, 3
Options 2 and 3 are the same magnitude with opposite sign — a candidate who computes I_B − I_A instead of I_A − I_B, or who forgets that the heavier sphere sits at the offset in I_B, lands exactly on option 2.
why it is trickyThree short steps of parallel-axis work, but the arithmetic itself is trivial because the two 25MR² and 25mr² self terms cancel; what carries the question is the sign, and option 2 is the precise wrong-order result, so the candidate cannot shortcut past the subtraction.
the catchA pure sign trap: the key and option 2 differ only in the order of subtraction, and option 4 punishes anyone who uses R − r instead of the touching-sphere separation R + r.

Q31Physics
subjectPhysics
streamnot applicable (Physics has no streams)
branchnot applicable (Physics has no branches)
chapterModern Physics high confidence
why this chapterThe answer comes from Bohr's model of the hydrogen atom, where the Coulomb force supplies the centripetal force (r = ke^2/(mv^2)), giving r = 1 x 10^-9 m.
subtopic 1Bohr's Atomic Model
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer comes from the Bohr-orbit balance of Coulomb force and centripetal force, r = ke^2/(mv^2) = 1e-9 m, which is taught under Bohr's atomic model.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Consider that an electron is revolving in an excited state of Hydrogen atom with velocity 25.6× 105 m s-1. The radius of the orbit is x× 10-9 m. The value of x is : (Take the mass of electron to be 9× 10-31 kg, charge of electron = -1.6× 10-19 C and 14πε0 = 9× 109 N m2 C-2 ]
  1. 1
  2. 4
  3. 3
  4. 2
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (1)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 1 · high
  1. For circular motion of the electron, mv2r = 14πε0e2r2.
  2. Rearranging, r = 14πε0e2mv2.
  3. Numerator: 9×109 × (1.6×10-19)2 = 9×109× 2.56×10-38 = 2.304×10-28.
  4. Denominator: mv2 = 9×10-31 × 25.6×1010 = 2.304×10-19.
  5. r = 2.304×10-282.304×10-19 = 1×10-9 m, so x = 1, which is option 1.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
11 highagree
difficultymedium · 3 steps
stepsRecognise that for the orbiting electron the Coulomb attraction supplies the centripetal force: mv2/r = ke2/r2, so r = ke2/(mv2) → Square the given velocity: v2 = 25.6 x 1010 = 2.56 x 1011 m2 s-2 → Evaluate numerator ke2 = 9e9 x (1.6e-19)2 = 2.304e-28 and denominator mv2 = 9e-31 x 2.56e11 = 2.304e-19, divide to get r in units of 10-9 m and read off x
confusing options2 of 4 · options 1, 4
The key is the clean result of the force balance, while '2' is the landing spot for the classic factor-of-two slip (using kinetic energy KE = ke2/2r, i.e. r = ke2/(2mv2) doubled, or mishandling the squared velocity) — '3' and '4-as-value' have no arithmetic route and are inert filler.
why it is trickyThree short steps with a single standard relation, but the work is all in the exponent bookkeeping: squaring a velocity given as a surd times 105, then dividing two numbers that differ by exactly nine powers of ten — the method is one line but the execution is where candidates lose it, and the nearby integer options give no sanity check since all four are plausible-looking small numbers.
the catchThe velocity is deliberately disguised as sqrt(25.6) x 10^5 so the square must be formed carefully (2.56 x 10^11, not 2.56 x 10^10 or 10^12); a one-decade slip or the energy-versus-force factor of two lands on a different integer option, and the integer options themselves offer no order-of-magnitude discrimination.

Q32Physics

chapterThermodynamics medium confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on the kinetic-theory mean free path relation lambda = 1/(sqrt(2) pi d^2 n), which this taxonomy lists under Thermodynamics (topic 'Mean Free Path, RMS, Average, and Most Probable Speed in Terms of Temperature').
subtopic 1Mean Free Path, RMS, Average, and Most Probable Speed in Terms of Temperature
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on the mean free path formula lambda = 1/(sqrt(2) pi d^2 n), which gives n_A = n_B/2 when lambda_A = lambda_B/2 and d_A = 2d_B.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
The mean free path of molecules in an ideal gas A is half that of another ideal gas B. The diameter of the spherical molecules of gas A is twice the diameter of the molecules of B. If number densities of the gases A and B are nA and nB, respectively, then the correct option is :
  1. nA = 12nB
  2. nA = nB
  3. nA = 2nB
  4. nA = 14nB
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (1)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 1 · high
  1. Mean free path: λ = 12 π d2 n.
  2. Ratio: λAλB = nB dB2nA dA2.
  3. With dA = 2dB: λAλB = nB4 nA.
  4. Given λAλB = 12: nB4nA = 12 ⇒ nA = 12 nB, which is option 1.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
11 highagree
difficultymedium · 3 steps
stepsRecall mean free path λ = 1/(√2 π d² n), i.e. λ ∝ 1/(d² n) → Form the ratio λ_A/λ_B = (d_B² n_B)/(d_A² n_A) and insert λ_A/λ_B = 1/2, d_A = 2d_B → Solve: n_B/(4 n_A) = 1/2 → n_A = n_B/2
confusing options2 of 4 · options 1, 2
Option 2 is exactly what a candidate gets who remembers λ ∝ 1/(d n) and forgets that the diameter is squared, so it is the natural wrong landing point; option 1 is the result with the square kept.
why it is trickyOnly two short steps of algebra once the formula is written, but the options are built around the single trap in that formula — the d² — so the question is not handed over: options 1 and 2 differ precisely by whether the diameter ratio is squared, and options 3 and 4 (factor inverted or λ ignored) fall away on a quick check.
the catchThe whole question turns on the square on the diameter in the mean free path formula; dropping it lands exactly on option 2, and a candidate who also inverts the λ ratio can talk themselves into option 4.

Q33Physics
subjectPhysics
streamnot applicable (Physics has no streams)
branchnot applicable (Physics has no branches)
chapterOscillations high confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on the time period of SHM of a floating body, T = 2π√(m/(Aρg)) so T ∝ 1/√ρ, giving ρ2/ρ1 = 1/4; buoyancy only supplies the restoring force.
subtopic 1Calculate Time Period of SHM
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on the time period of a floating cork's SHM, T = 2*pi*sqrt(m/(rho*A*g)), so T is proportional to 1/sqrt(rho) and doubling T gives rho2/rho1 = 1/4.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
A cylindrical cork of uniform density floats in a liquid of density ρ1. If the cork is depressed slightly and released, it oscillates harmonically with time period T. If the same cork floats in another liquid of density ρ2, then the similar oscillation has time period 2T. The value of ρ2/ρ1 is :
  1. 14
  2. 4
  3. 2
  4. 12
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (1)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 1 · high
  1. For a depression x, the extra buoyant force is the restoring force: F = -ρ A g x.
  2. So k = ρ A g and T = 2πmρ A g, giving T ∝ 1ρ (same cork, so m and A are fixed).
  3. T2T1 = ρ1ρ2 = 2.
  4. ρ2ρ1 = 14, which is option 1.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
11 highagree
difficultymedium · 2 steps
stepsRecall/derive the restoring force on a depressed floating cylinder: F = -(A ρ_liquid g) x, so ω² = A ρ_liquid g / m with m fixed (same cork). → Hence T ∝ 1/√ρ_liquid; set 2T/T = √(ρ_1/ρ_2) = 2 and solve the ratio ρ_2/ρ_1.
confusing options2 of 4 · options 1, 2
Option 1 is the correct inverse-square result, while option 2 (4) is exactly what a candidate gets who writes T ∝ √ρ instead of 1/√ρ — the same number with the dependence flipped; option 4 12 is the leftover of forgetting to square and option 3 (2) is its mirror, both far less tempting once the √ is written down.
why it is trickyShort to execute — one standard proportionality and one ratio — but it is not easy, because the four options are built as the two sign-of-exponent errors and the two forgot-to-square errors, so nothing is eliminated on sight and only correctly getting T ∝ 1/√ρ_liquid picks between 1/4 and 4.
the catchThe whole question turns on the direction of the dependence: denser liquid gives a stiffer restoring force and hence a shorter period, and a candidate who mis-places ρ lands squarely on option 2.

Q34Physics
subjectPhysics
streamnot applicable (Physics has no streams)
branchnot applicable (Physics has no branches)
chapterWaves high confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on counting the nodes of standing sound waves in open and closed organ pipes for given harmonics, which is taught under Organ Pipe in Waves.
subtopic 1Organ Pipe
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on counting displacement nodes for harmonics of open and closed organ pipes (5 nodes each, giving n/m = 1), which is taught under Organ Pipe.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
For sound waves, if the number of nodes for the 5th harmonic of an open-ended pipe is n and that for the 9th harmonic of the same pipe with one of its ends closed is m, the ratio nm is :
  1. 35
  2. 59
  3. 95
  4. 1
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (4)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 4 · high
  1. For an open pipe the pth harmonic has L = pλ2 with antinodes at both ends, giving p nodes.
  2. So for the 5th harmonic of the open pipe, n = 5.
  3. For a closed pipe L = (2k-1)λ4, with a node at the closed end and an antinode at the open end, giving k nodes.
  4. The 9th harmonic means 2k-1 = 9, so k = 5 and m = 5.
  5. nm = 55 = 1, which is option 4.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
44 highagree
difficultymedium · 3 steps
stepsFor an open-open pipe, the 5th harmonic means L = 5λ/2, so count the nodes: 5 nodes (n = 5). → For a closed pipe only odd harmonics exist; the 9th harmonic means L = 9λ/4, so count nodes from the closed end at 0, λ/2, λ, 3λ/2, 2λ → 5 nodes (m = 5). → Form n/m.
confusing options2 of 4 · options 2, 4
Option 2 59 is exactly what a candidate gets by writing the harmonic numbers themselves as the node counts, which is the single commonest slip here; option 4 is what the actual node count gives, and options 1 and 3 are only reciprocal/garbled variants that nobody reaches by a clean route.
why it is trickyShort to execute — two node counts and a ratio — but it is not easy, because the plausible-looking 5/9 sits right next to the key and only the explicit node counting in the closed pipe (where the 9th harmonic has (9+1)/2 = 5 nodes, not 9) separates them.
the catchThe trap is treating 'harmonic number' as 'number of nodes'; the closed-pipe odd-harmonic numbering is not signposted in the stem and must be supplied by the candidate.

subjectPhysics
