chapterModern Physics high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on the Q value of alpha decay from the mass defect and mass-energy equivalence (0.004 u x 931.5 MeV = 3726 keV), which Modern Physics teaches under its Alpha Decay, Mass Defect and Mass-Energy Equivalence topics.
subtopic 1Alpha, Beta, and Gamma Decay
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer is the Q value of the alpha decay of U-238, (238.050 - 234.043 - 4.003) u x 931.5 MeV/u = 3726 keV, which is taught under alpha decay energetics.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Consider the following nuclear reaction : 238U → 234Th + 4He Take masses of 238U, 234Th and 4He as 238.050 u, 234.043 u and 4.003 u, respectively. The Q value for the reaction, in keV, is : [Given : 1 u = 931.5 MeV c-2]
  1. 3740
  2. 3726
  3. 3730
  4. 3736
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (2)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · high
  1. Δ m = mU - (mTh + mHe) = 238.050 - (234.043 + 4.003) u.
  2. Δ m = 238.050 - 238.046 = 0.004 u.
  3. Q = Δ m × 931.5 MeV/u = 0.004 × 931.5 = 3.726 MeV.
  4. 3.726 MeV = 3726 keV, which is option 2.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
22 highagree
difficultymedium · 2 steps
stepsTake mass defect: 238.050 - (234.043 + 4.003) = 238.050 - 238.046 = 0.004 u → Convert: 0.004 x 931.5 MeV = 3.726 MeV = 3726 keV
confusing options2 of 4 · options 2, 3
All four options sit within 15 keV of each other, so no one can be thrown out on magnitude; the only serious rival to 3726 is 3730, which is what a candidate gets by rounding the product (or rounding 931.5 to 930-ish) instead of carrying the exact 0.004 x 931.5.
why it is trickyTwo short steps and the method is completely signposted, but the options are deliberately clustered so elimination fails entirely — the question is decided purely by whether the small difference of two near-equal four-decimal masses and the multiplication are carried exactly, which is the classic NEET arithmetic-slip trap rather than a conceptual one.
the catchSubtracting 238.046 from 238.050 is a difference of near-equal large numbers where one dropped decimal changes the answer by hundreds of keV, and every distractor lies inside that slip range.

Q38Physics
subjectPhysics

chapterUnits and Measurements high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on combining the dimensional formulae of the three constants ([MT^-3K^-4], [ML^2T^-2K^-1], [LK]) to get [L^-1T^-1K^-2], a dimensional-analysis skill taught in Units and Measurements; the radiation-law context is only the dressing.
subtopic 1Dimensional Formulae
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on combining the dimensional formulae of the Stefan-Boltzmann constant [MT^-3K^-4], Boltzmann constant [ML^2T^-2K^-1] and Wien's constant [LK] to get [L^-1T^-1K^-2].
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Consider that σs, kB, b represent Stefan-Boltzmann constant, Boltzmann constant and Wien's displacement law constant, respectively. The dimension of σs kB-1 b is:
  1. [L-1T-1K-4]
  2. [L-1T-1K-2]
  3. [L-1K-2]
  4. [L-1T-1K-3]
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (2)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · high
  1. σs = PA T4 ⇒ [σs] = [M L2 T-3][L2][K4] = [M T-3 K-4].
  2. kB = ET ⇒ [kB] = [M L2 T-2 K-1], so [kB-1] = [M-1 L-2 T2 K].
  3. b = λm T ⇒ [b] = [L K].
  4. [σs kB-1] = [M T-3 K-4][M-1 L-2 T2 K] = [L-2 T-1 K-3].
  5. [σs kB-1 b] = [L-2 T-1 K-3][L K] = [L-1 T-1 K-2], which is option 2.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
22 highagree
difficultyhard · 4 steps
stepsWrite σ_s from Stefan's law E = σT⁴: [M T⁻³ K⁻⁴] → Write k_B from E = k_B T: [M L² T⁻² K⁻¹] → Write b from λ_m T = b: [L K] → Divide and multiply, cancelling M and collecting L, T, K exponents: (M T⁻³K⁻⁴)/(M L²T⁻²K⁻¹) × L K
confusing options3 of 4 · options 1, 2, 4
All three carry the same [L⁻¹T⁻¹] and differ only in the power of K — K⁻⁴ is what you get if you forget to divide by k_B's K⁻¹, K⁻³ if you forget b's single K, and only the full bookkeeping gives K⁻²; option 3 is dropped on sight because it has no time dimension at all.
why it is trickyFour steps — three separate dimensional recalls (Stefan, Boltzmann, Wien) plus a combination — and the three near-identical options differ only in the temperature exponent, so elimination is useless and the candidate must get every K power right.
the catchThe whole question turns on the K exponent arithmetic (−4 + 1 + 1 = −2); each common slip in that bookkeeping lands exactly on a printed distractor, and the mass dimension cancelling can lull a candidate into skipping k_B's K⁻¹.

Q39Physics
subjectPhysics
streamnot applicable (Physics has no streams)
branchnot applicable (Physics has no branches)
chapterModern Physics high confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on the photoelectric effect: no emission when the incident wavelength exceeds the threshold (V1 = 0), and a larger stopping potential the further the threshold wavelength exceeds the incident wavelength (V2 < V3).
subtopic 1Photoelectric Effect
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on the threshold-wavelength condition for photoemission (no emission, so zero stopping potential, when lambda exceeds the threshold) and on Einstein's photoelectric equation, under which a larger threshold wavelength gives a larger stopping potential.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
A ray of light with wavelength λ is incident on three different photo-electric cells namely 1, 2 and 3. The threshold wavelength of these photo-electric cells are λ1, λ2 and λ3, respectively and the magnitude of stopping potentials of these cells are V1, V2 and V3, respectively. The relation between λ and threshold wavelengths are λ1 < λ, λ2 > λ and λ3 >> λ. The correct option is:
  1. V1 < V2, V3 = 0
  2. V1 = 0, V2 < V3
  3. V1 = 0, V2 > V3
  4. V1 > V2, V3 = 0
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (2)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · high
  1. Photoemission happens only if λ < λth. For cell 1, λ1 < λ, so there is no emission and V1 = 0.
  2. For cells 2 and 3, eVi = hc(1λ - 1λi).
  3. λ3 ≫ λ and λ2 > λ (with λ3 > λ2), so 1λ3 < 1λ2 and therefore V3 > V2.
  4. V1 = 0, V2 < V3, which is option 2.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
22 highagree
difficultyhard · 3 steps
stepsRecall the emission condition in wavelength form: photoemission occurs only if the incident λ is SHORTER than the threshold wavelength λ0. → Apply it to cell 1 (λ1 < λ, i.e. incident wavelength exceeds threshold) → no emission → V1 = 0; cells 2 and 3 (λ2 > λ, λ3 >> λ) do emit. → Compare stopping potentials using eV = hc(1/λ − 1/λ0): the larger λ0 gives the smaller 1/λ0 and hence the larger V, so V3 > V2.
confusing options3 of 4 · options 2, 3, 4
Option 3 is the exact sign-flip of the key — it keeps V1 = 0 but reverses the V2/V3 comparison, which is what a student gets by assuming a bigger threshold wavelength means a smaller stopping potential; option 4 is what a student gets by applying the frequency-form condition to wavelengths (treating λ1 < λ as the emitting case and λ3 >> λ as no emission), a very common reversal.
why it is trickyThree short steps, but the options do no eliminating at all: the four choices are the two possible answers to step 2 crossed with the two possible answers to step 3, so a candidate who reverses either the emission inequality or the 1/λ − 1/λ0 comparison lands exactly on a printed option.
the catchThe whole question turns on the threshold condition being stated in WAVELENGTH rather than frequency, so the familiar 'greater than threshold → emission' habit gives the wrong cell; and the second comparison needs the 1/λ0 term, not λ0 itself.

chapterUnits and Measurements high confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on the least count and zero error correction of a Vernier calliper, which is taught in Units and Measurements.
subtopic 1Vernier Calliper and Screw Gauge
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on finding the negative zero error of a Vernier calliper from the coinciding Vernier division and correcting the measured reading with it.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
One main scale division of a Vernier calliper is equal to 1 mm and the number of divisions on the Vernier scale is 10. When both the jaws touch each other, the Vernier scale shifts to the left of zero of the main scale in such a way that 4th Vernier division coincides with a division of the main scale. If this Vernier caliper measures the length of a wire to be 1 cm, the actual length of the wire is:
  1. 1.04 cm
  2. 0.60 cm
  3. 0.96 cm
  4. 1.00 cm
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (none)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 1 · medium
  1. Least count = 1 mm10 = 0.1 mm.
  2. Vernier zero lies to the left of main-scale zero, so the zero error is negative.
  3. Taking the coinciding division directly, zero error = -4 × 0.1 mm = -0.04 cm.
  4. Actual length = measured - zero error = 1.00 - (-0.04) = 1.04 cm, which is option 1.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
—1 mediumunchecked
difficultyhard · 3 steps
stepsLeast count = 1 MSD / N = 1 mm / 10 = 0.1 mm = 0.01 cm. → Identify the zero error: vernier zero lies to the LEFT of main-scale zero with the 4th vernier division coinciding, so the error is negative, of magnitude 4 x 0.1 mm = 0.4 mm = 0.04 cm. → Correct the reading: actual length = observed reading - (zero error) = 1.00 cm + 0.04 cm.
confusing options2 of 4 · options 1, 3
Options 1 and 3 are the same 0.04 cm correction applied with opposite signs — 1.04 cm if you recognise a negative zero error that must be added back, 0.96 cm if you reflexively subtract the error as you would for a right-shifted vernier zero; option 4 is the uncorrected reading and option 2 is far off in magnitude.
why it is trickyThree short steps, but the options are built as a sign trap: the whole question turns on which side of the main-scale zero the vernier zero has shifted, and 0.96 cm sits there waiting for anyone who applies the correction the usual way, so elimination cannot rescue a candidate who gets the convention wrong.
the catchNegative zero error with a left-shifted vernier — candidates also argue over whether the magnitude is 4 x LC or (10 - 4) x LC, and the sign error lands exactly on a printed option.

Q41Physics
subjectPhysics

chapterElectrostatics high confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on the electrostatics of conductors: the field inside the cavity is nonzero due to Q, while the induced charge on the outer surface of the isolated conducting sphere spreads uniformly, so the field outside is symmetric (E_B = E_C).
subtopic 1Electrostatic Shielding
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer rests on the conductor shielding the outside from the cavity charge: the induced charge on the outer surface spreads uniformly whatever Q's position, so E_B = E_C, while the field inside the cavity at A is nonzero.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
A point charge Q is placed inside a cavity within a solid isolated conducting sphere. Consider points A, B and C as shown in the figure, where the magnitudes of the electric fields are EA, EB and EC, respectively. The points B and C are at the same distance from the center of the solid sphere. The correct option is:
  1. EA ≠ 0, EB < EC
  2. EA = 0, EB = EC
  3. EA ≠ 0, EB = EC
  4. EA = 0, EB > EC
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (3)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 3 · high
  1. Point A lies inside the cavity alongside Q, where the field due to Q and the induced -Q on the cavity wall does not vanish, so EA ≠ 0.
  2. Charge +Q is induced on the outer surface; the conductor shields the cavity, so this charge spreads uniformly over the outer sphere.
  3. Outside the sphere E = kQr2 measured from the centre; since rB = rC, EB = EC, which is option 3.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
33 highagree
difficultyhard · 2 steps
stepsLocate A: it lies inside the cavity, where the point charge's own field is unscreened, so E_A ≠ 0 (the shielding 'E = 0' applies only to the metal bulk). → Note the sphere is isolated: the induced +Q spreads uniformly over the OUTER surface irrespective of where the off-centre cavity sits, so the external field is spherically symmetric and E_B = E_C at equal radii.
confusing options3 of 4 · options 1, 2, 3
Option 2 is what a candidate gets by firing off the reflex 'field inside a conductor is zero' without checking that A sits in the cavity, not the metal; option 1 is what you get by reasoning geometrically from the figure — the cavity and charge are visibly nearer B than C, so the field 'should' be stronger on that side — which ignores the uniform redistribution on the outer surface; only option 3 survives both checks, and option 4 combines both errors so almost nobody picks it.
why it is trickyOnly two steps and no arithmetic at all, but both steps are traps rather than calculations, and two genuinely attractive wrong routes (the mis-read of A's position and the figure-driven asymmetry argument) each land exactly on a printed option — a careless but prepared candidate has three live choices and no numerical sanity check to fall back on.
the catchThe figure deliberately draws the cavity off-centre, inviting the false inference that the external field is stronger on the B side; the key insight — that charge on the outer surface of an isolated conductor is uniform regardless of cavity placement — is nowhere signposted in the stem.

chapterModern Physics high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on the angular distribution of alpha particles in Rutherford (Geiger-Marsden) scattering, where N(theta) falls steeply with angle, which is taught under alpha-particle scattering in Modern Physics.
subtopic 1Alpha-Particle Scattering and Rutherford Nuclear Model of the Atom and Thomson's Model
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on how the number of scattered alpha-particles varies with scattering angle in the Geiger-Marsden experiment, which is taught under alpha-particle scattering and Rutherford's nuclear model.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
In Geiger-Marsden experiment, the number of scattered α-particles N(θ) is plotted as a function of scattering angle θ. Which of the following options represents the correct plot?
  1. drawn by the pipeline from a figure spec
  2. drawn by the pipeline from a figure spec
  3. drawn by the pipeline from a figure spec
  4. drawn by the pipeline from a figure spec
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (4)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 4 · high
  1. Rutherford scattering formula: N(θ) ∝ 1sin4(θ/2).
  2. As θ → 0, sin(θ/2) → 0, so N(θ) is very large; most α-particles pass almost undeflected.
  3. N(θ) decreases monotonically with θ; N(10°)N(180°) = 1sin4 5° ≈ 1.7× 104, and most of this fall happens at small angles, so the curve drops steeply then levels off.
  4. Options 1 (peak at 90°) and 2 (increasing) are wrong; option 3 stays nearly flat at small angles, contrary to the formula; option 4 shows the steep fall and flat tail, so the answer is option 4.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
44 highagree
difficultymedium · 1 step
stepsRecall the Rutherford/Geiger-Marsden result that N(θ) ∝ 1/sin⁴(θ/2) — the count falls steeply and monotonically from small angles, with only a tiny fraction scattered at large θ — and match that shape to the plotted curve on the logarithmic N-axis (drop of several decades by ~90°, then a flat tail)
confusing options2 of 4 · options 3, 4
Both 3 and 4 are monotonically decreasing curves starting at 10⁷; 3 is a gentle, nearly flat fall that only loses a few decades near 180°, while 4 shows the sharp 1/sin⁴(θ/2) collapse within the first 30-60° and a long flat tail — only knowing how violently the cross-section drops separates them.
why it is trickyOne recalled graph, but it is not easy: two of the four options are decreasing curves of the right general family, and the candidate who only remembers 'fewer particles at larger angles' without the steepness of 1/sin⁴(θ/2) can land on option 3; options 1 (peak at 90°) and 2 (rising) are eliminated on sight.
the catchThe rival distractor is the same qualitative shape with the wrong rate of fall — the question turns on remembering the magnitude of the drop (orders of magnitude within the first few tens of degrees) rather than just the direction of the trend.

chapterThermodynamics high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on the first law for a cyclic process (zero change in internal energy, so net heat supplied equals net work, the area enclosed by the cycle), which is taught in thermodynamics.
subtopic 1Polytropic Process and Cyclic Process
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicFor a cyclic process ΔU = 0, so the net heat supplied equals the net work, the area enclosed by the p-V loop (200 N/m² × 3 m³ = 600 J).
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
One mole of an ideal monatomic gas undergoes a cyclic process as shown in the figure. The total heat supplied to the gas is :
  1. 800 J
  2. 400 J
  3. 500 J
  4. 600 J
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (4)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 4 · high
  1. For a complete cycle Δ U = 0, so Qnet = Wnet.
  2. The cycle a → b → c → d → a is clockwise on the p-V diagram, so Wnet is positive and equals the enclosed area.
  3. Wnet = (300 - 100) N m-2 × (5 - 2) m3 = 200 × 3 = 600 J.
  4. Qnet = 600 J, which is option 4.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
44 highagree
difficultyeasy · 2 steps
stepsRecognise the process is cyclic, so ΔU = 0 and Q_total = W_net. → Compute W_net as the enclosed rectangular area: (300 − 100) N/m² × (5 − 2) m³ = 600 J, positive since the loop is clockwise.
confusing options2 of 4 · options 4, 2
600 J is the full rectangle area; 400 J is exactly what a careless reader gets by taking ΔV as 2 m³ (the left-hand coordinate) instead of 5 − 2 = 3, while 800 J and 500 J match no plausible slip in this geometry.
why it is trickyEasy: the whole question is one recalled idea (ΔU = 0 round a cycle, so Q = enclosed area) followed by a single multiplication of two small differences read straight off the graph — the figure hands over both the pressure and volume gaps, and only one arithmetic route gives a number in the list.
the catchThe only real trap is misreading the volume axis difference (3 m³, not 2), which lands exactly on option 2; the monatomic detail is a red herring since Cv never enters for a closed cycle.

Q44Physics
subjectPhysics
streamnot applicable (Physics has no streams)
branchnot applicable (Physics has no branches)
chapterMagnetic Effect of Electric Current high confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on the force per unit length between two parallel current-carrying wires, mu0*I*(2I)/(2*pi*h), balanced against the weight per unit length lambda*g.
subtopic 1Force Between Two Parallel Current-Carrying Conductors
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer comes from equating the attractive force per unit length between parallel currents, mu0(I)(2I)/(2*pi*h), to the weight per unit length lambda*g, giving h = mu0 I^2/(pi lambda g).
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Two infinitely long parallel conducting wires A and B carry currents I and 2I, respectively, in the same direction. The wire A has uniform mass per unit length λ and lies on an insulated floor. The wire B is kept fixed at a height h above the floor. The minimum magnitude of h so that the wire A does not rise from the floor is :
[ g is the acceleration due to gravity and μ0 is the permeability of free space.]
  1. 4μ0 I2πλ g
  2. μ0 I22πλ g
  3. μ0 I2πλ g
  4. 2μ0 I2πλ g
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (3)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 3 · high
  1. Currents in the same direction attract, so wire B above pulls wire A upward.
  2. Force per unit length on A: FL = μ0 I (2I)2π h = μ0 I2π h.
  3. Weight per unit length of A is λ g; A does not rise if μ0 I2π h ≤ λ g.
  4. h ≥ μ0 I2π λ g, so hmin = μ0 I2π λ g, which is option 3.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
33 highagree
difficultyhard · 3 steps
stepsRecall force per unit length between parallel wires: F/L = μ₀I₁I₂/(2πh), attractive for parallel currents — wire B above pulls A up. → Substitute I₁ = I, I₂ = 2I to get F/L = μ₀(2I²)/(2πh) = μ₀I²/(πh). → Set this equal to the weight per unit length λg for the just-about-to-lift condition and solve h = μ₀I²/(πλg).
confusing options3 of 4 · options 2, 3, 4
All four options are the same expression differing only by a numerical factor, so every arithmetic slip lands exactly on an option: forgetting that B carries 2I gives option 2, cancelling the 2π against the wrong 2 gives option 4, and only careful bookkeeping of the two separate factors of 2 reaches option 3.
why it is trickyThree short steps, but elimination is impossible — the options are identical in form and differ only by the factor 2 that the problem buries in two places (the 2I current and the 2π in the force formula), so two genuine factor-of-two rivals sit right next to the key and the question discriminates purely on that bookkeeping.
the catchClassic NEET factor-of-two trap: the current 2I and the 2π denominator both carry a 2, and each way of mishandling them maps onto a printed option; dimensional or order-of-magnitude checking gives no help since all options have identical dimensions.

Q45Physics
subjectPhysics
streamnot applicable (Physics has no streams)
branchnot applicable (Physics has no branches)
chapterOscillations high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on energy conservation in SHM, (1/2)mv^2 + (1/2)kx^2 = constant, which gives a circle in the x-v plane only when k = m.
subtopic 1Velocity in SHM
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on the velocity-displacement relation v = ω√(A² − x²), which traces a circle on the x-v plane only when ω = √(k/m) = 1, giving k = m.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Consider a spring-mass simple harmonic oscillator in one dimension. The mass of the particle is m kg and the spring constant is k N m-1. At a given instant, the extension of the spring is x meter and the speed of the particle is v m s-1. On the x-v plane, if the graph of v as a function of x is a circle, then the correct option is:
  1. k=m
  2. k=1m
  3. k=m
  4. k=m2
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (3)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 3 · high
  1. Total mechanical energy is conserved: 12kx2 + 12mv2 = E.
  2. Multiply by 2: kx2 + mv2 = 2E, an ellipse in the x-v plane.
  3. A circle x2 + v2 = r2 needs equal coefficients, so k = m (numerically), which is option 3.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
33 highagree
difficultyhard · 3 steps
stepsWrite energy conservation for the oscillator: ½mv² + ½kx² = E (constant). → Rearrange to x²/(2E/k) + v²/(2E/m) = 1 and recognise this as an ellipse in the x–v plane with semi-axes √(2E/k) along x and √(2E/m) along v. → Impose the circle condition (equal semi-axes): 2E/k = 2E/m, i.e. ω = √(k/m) = 1, giving the relation between k and m.
confusing options3 of 4 · options 1, 3, 4
The key follows from ω = √(k/m) = 1; option 4 (k = m²) is exactly what a candidate gets by squaring the condition on the wrong side (setting √k/m = 1), and option 1 (k = √m) comes from setting k/√m = 1 — both are the same method with one algebraic slip, while option 2 is the only one no plausible route reaches.
why it is trickyThree steps, and the answer k = m is dimensionally odd-looking, so candidates who half-remember ω = √(k/m) hesitate and the two square-root-slip options (1 and 4) stay genuinely live right to the end; elimination gives no help because all four are simple power relations between the same two symbols.
the catchThe unsignposted insight that the v-versus-x phase plot of an SHM is an ellipse whose semi-axes are A and ωA, so 'circle' is a disguised statement that ω = 1 — a candidate who tries to graph v(x) directly without the energy equation has no route at all.

chapterAldehydes, Ketones, and Carboxylic Acids high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on Etard reaction of toluene giving benzaldehyde (P), which is also obtained by Rosenmund reduction of benzoyl chloride with Pd/BaSO4 - both preparations of aldehydes.
subtopic 1Preparation of Benzaldehyde
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on recognising that toluene with CrO2Cl2/CS2 then H3O+ (Etard reaction) gives benzaldehyde, which is also obtained by Rosenmund reduction of benzoyl chloride with Pd/BaSO4.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Consider the following reaction, and choose the correct option.
  1. Compound P is obtained by the hydrogenation of benzoyl chloride with Pd on BaSO4.
  2. On treating compound P with saturated NaHCO3 solution, brisk effervescence is observed.
  3. Compound P can be prepared by treating benzene with anhydrous AlCl3 and CH3COCl.
  4. On treatment with bromine water, compound P gives a white precipitate.
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (1)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 1 · high
  1. Toluene with CrO2Cl2 in CS2 followed by H3O+ is the Etard reaction, giving benzaldehyde, so P = C6H5CHO.
  2. Rosenmund reduction: C6H5COCl H2, Pd/BaSO4  C6H5CHO, so option 1 is correct.
  3. Benzaldehyde is not acidic enough to liberate CO2 from NaHCO3, so option 2 is false.
  4. Friedel-Crafts acylation of benzene with CH3COCl/AlCl3 gives acetophenone, not benzaldehyde, so option 3 is false.
  5. The white precipitate with bromine water is the test for phenol (2,4,6-tribromophenol), not benzaldehyde, so option 4 is false; the answer is option 1.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
11 highagree
difficultyhard · 5 steps
stepsRecognise CrO2Cl2/CS2 followed by H3O+ as the Étard reaction, giving benzaldehyde (not benzoic acid) from toluene → Check option 1: recall Rosenmund reduction, PhCOCl + H2/Pd-BaSO4 gives benzaldehyde → Check option 2: NaHCO3 effervescence requires a carboxylic acid, not an aldehyde → Check option 3: Friedel-Crafts with CH3COCl gives acetophenone, a ketone, not the aldehyde → Check option 4: white precipitate with bromine water is the phenol/aniline test, not an aldehyde test
confusing options3 of 4 · options 1, 2, 4
Option 2 is exactly what a candidate gets who lets CrO2Cl2 over-oxidise toluene to benzoic acid — the single commonest error on the Étard reaction; option 4 tempts anyone who remembers 'bromine water gives white ppt' as a generic aromatic test; option 1 requires knowing Rosenmund stops cleanly at the aldehyde and is only rejected if you doubt the Pd/BaSO4 poisoning.
why it is trickyHard: it is really five recalls in one question — identify the Étard product, then run four independent named-reaction checks (Rosenmund, NaHCO3 test, Friedel-Crafts acylation, bromine-water test) — and elimination fails because three options stay live until each is separately tested.
the catchThe whole item hangs on the reaction CONDITION: CrO2Cl2 in CS2 with aqueous workup stops at the aldehyde, whereas the usual chromium oxidation of toluene would go to the acid — and option 2 is precisely the acid-product distractor. Long to execute even though each individual check is one line.

chapterCoordination Compounds – Practice high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on translating an IUPAC name of a coordination compound into its formula (ligands inside the coordination sphere, counter-ions outside), which is coordination compound nomenclature.
subtopic 1IUPAC Nomenclature and Formulae of Coordination Compounds
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on converting the IUPAC name tetraammineaquachloridocobalt(III) chloride into its formula [Co(NH3)4(H2O)Cl]Cl2, which is IUPAC nomenclature and formula writing.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
The formula of tetraammineaquachloridocobalt(III) chloride is
  1. [Co(NH3)4(H2O)Cl]Cl2
  2. [Co(NH3)4Cl2]× H2O
  3. [Co(NH3)4]Cl3× H2O
  4. [Co(NH3)4(H2O)Cl]Cl
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (1)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 1 · high
  1. The name lists the ligands inside the coordination sphere as tetraammine (4 NH3), aqua (H2O) and chlorido (Cl-), with cobalt in the +3 state and chloride as the counter ion.
  2. Charge on the complex ion: +3 + 4(0) + 0 + (-1) = +2, so the ion is [Co(NH3)4(H2O)Cl]2+.

printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
11 highagree
difficultymedium · 3 steps
stepsTranslate the name into the coordination sphere: 4 NH3, 1 H2O, 1 chlorido ligand around Co(III) → Compute the complex-ion charge: +3 from cobalt minus 1 from coordinated chloride = +2 → Balance with chloride counter ions outside the brackets: 2 Cl-

Options 1 and 4 have the identical, correctly named coordination sphere and differ only in the number of outer-sphere chlorides, so a candidate who forgets that the coordinated Cl- cancels one unit of Co(III) charge lands exactly on 4.
why it is trickyMedium: the naming part is routine and options 2 and 3 die on sight (they omit the aqua ligand from the sphere and use a meaningless '×H2O' hydrate notation), but the real work is the charge balance, and option 4 is precisely the result of the commonest slip — counting Co(III) as +1 net or ignoring the inner chloride.

chapterThe D- and F-Block Elements – Practice high confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on the 4f electronic configurations of Ln3+ ions (Ho3+ is 4f10 with four unpaired electrons), which is taught under lanthanoids in the d- and f-block chapter.
subtopic 1Electronic Configurations of the d-Block & f-Block
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on writing the 4f configurations of the Ln3+ ions (Ho3+ is 4f10, giving four unpaired electrons), which is taught under electronic configurations of f-block elements.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
The lanthanide ion having four unpaired electrons is (Given: Atomic numbers of Ce = 58, Nd = 60, Tb = 65 and Ho = 67)
  1. Ho3+
  2. Nd3+
  3. Ce3+
  4. Tb3+
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (1)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 1 · high
  1. A Ln3+ ion has configuration [Xe] 4f Z-57.
  2. Ce3+ (Z=58): 4f1, so 1 unpaired electron.
  3. Nd3+ (Z=60): 4f3, so 3 unpaired electrons.
  4. Tb3+ (Z=65): 4f8, so 7-1 = 6 unpaired electrons.
  5. Ho3+ (Z=67): 4f10, so 14-10 = 4 unpaired electrons.
  6. Only Ho3+ has 4 unpaired electrons, which is option 1.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
11 highagree
difficultyhard · 4 steps
stepsFor each option subtract 3 from the atomic number to get the M(3+) electron count → Strip the [Xe] core (54 e) to get the 4f occupancy: Ce3+ 4f1, Nd3+ 4f3, Tb3+ 4f8, Ho3+ 4f10 → Apply Hund's rule across the seven f orbitals to count unpaired electrons (1, 3, 6, 4 respectively) → Pick the ion with exactly four unpaired electrons
confusing options2 of 4 · options 1, 2
Ho3+ (4f10) is the key, and Nd3+ is the real rival because the neutral atom is [Xe]4f4 6s2 — a candidate who removes only the 6s electrons and a 5d that isn't there, or who reads the neutral configuration, lands on 4f4 and 'four unpaired'; Ce3+ (4f1) and Tb3+ (4f8, six unpaired) are too far off to tempt anyone who writes a configuration at all.
why it is trickyHard: four counts with a live rival — the options do not narrow anything (all four are lanthanide 3+ ions and each must be configured before it can be discarded), and Nd3+ sits exactly on the wrong route of reading the neutral 4f4 instead of the tripositive 4f3, while the key Ho3+ needs the less obvious step of counting unpaired electrons in a more-than-half-filled 4f10 shell.
the catchThe Nd3+ trap: forgetting that the third electron comes out of 4f (not 5d/6s alone) turns 4f3 into 4f4 and gives exactly the asked-for four unpaired electrons.

Q49Chemistry / physical
subjectChemistry
streamphysical
branchnot applicable (Chemistry has no branches)
chapterChemical Kinetics high confidence
why this chapterThe answer is found by applying the Arrhenius equation (ln k = ln A - Ea/RT) read from the Arrhenius plot to find the temperature for a given rate constant, which is taught in Chemical Kinetics.
subtopic 1Arrhenius Equation
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer comes from reading the intercept (ln A = 6) off the Arrhenius plot and solving ln k = ln A - Ea/RT for T, which is the Arrhenius equation.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
For an elementary chemical reaction, the Arrhenius plot is given below. If the energy of activation is 6.64 kJ mol-1 and R = 8.3 J K-1 mol-1, the temperature at which the rate constant becomes e2 min-1, is:
  1. 250 K
  2. 125 K
  3. 150 K
  4. 200 K
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (4)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 4 · high
  1. The Arrhenius plot follows ln k = ln A - EaRT, so the intercept on the ln k axis is ln A = 6.
  2. For k = e2 min-1, ln k = 2, so 2 = 6 - EaRT.
  3. EaRT = 4.
  4. T = Ea4R = 66404 × 8.3 = 664033.2 = 200 K, which is option 4.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
44 highagree
difficultymedium · 3 steps
stepsRecognise the plot as ln k = ln A − (Ea/R)(1/T), so the y-intercept at 1/T = 0 gives ln A = 6 → Set ln k = 2 (since k = e² min⁻¹) and write 2 = 6 − Ea/(RT), i.e. Ea/(RT) = 4 → Solve T = 6640/(4 × 8.3) = 200 K
confusing options2 of 4 · options 4, 2
200 K comes from reading the intercept correctly as 6; 125 K is roughly what a candidate gets by misreading the intercept as 8 (6640/(6×8.3) ≈ 133), while 250 K and 150 K correspond to no clean slip and give untidy ln k values.
why it is trickyMedium: only three short steps and the arithmetic is clean, but the key datum — ln A = 6 — is not given in words and has to be extracted from the graph's intercept, and a careless read of the axis lands on a different option.
the catchThe intercept must be read off the plot rather than taken from the stem, and the natural-log form (no 2.303 factor) must be used; a candidate who inserts 2.303 or misreads the intercept gets a wrong but offered-looking value.

Q51Chemistry / organic
subjectChemistry
streamorganic
branchnot applicable (Chemistry has no branches)
chapterHydrocarbons high confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on the stereochemical course of alkene reactions of but-2-ene (anti addition of Br2 in CCl4 and syn dihydroxylation by alkaline KMnO4), which is taught under alkenes in Hydrocarbons.
subtopic 1Chemical Properties of Alkenes
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on the stereochemical outcome of alkene reactions: anti addition of Br2 to trans-but-2-ene and syn dihydroxylation of cis-but-2-ene by alkaline KMnO4.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Given below are two statements:
In the light of the above statements, choose the most appropriate answer from the options given below.
Statement-Itrans-But-2-ene upon treatment with Br2 in CCl4 gives the following product
Statement-IIcis-But-2-ene upon treatment with alkaline KMnO4 gives the following product.
  1. Statement I is incorrect but Statement II is correct
  2. Both Statement I and Statement II are correct
  3. Both Statement I and Statement II are incorrect
  4. Statement I is correct but Statement II is incorrect
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (1)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 1 · medium
  1. Anti addition of Br2 to trans-but-2-ene gives meso-2,3-dibromobutane.
  2. In the given Newman projection the front carbon has Br (up), H (lower left), Me (lower right); the back carbon has Br (down), H (upper left), Me (upper right).
  3. With the two Br anti, the front H is anti to the back Me, so the conformer has no centre of inversion; it is the chiral (dl) isomer, not meso.
  4. Hence Statement I is incorrect.
  5. Cold alkaline KMnO4 adds two OH groups syn; syn addition to cis-but-2-ene gives meso-butane-2,3-diol, so Statement II (if it shows the meso diol) is correct.
  6. Statement I incorrect, Statement II correct: option 1.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
11 mediumagree
difficultyhard · 5 steps
stepsRecall that Br2/CCl4 adds anti across a C=C, so trans-but-2-ene must give the meso-2,3-dibromobutane (and cis would give the d/l pair). → Decode the first Newman projection: front carbon carries Br (up), H (lower-left), Me (lower-right); back carbon carries Br (down), H and Me at the upper positions. → Assign configuration at both stereocentres of that projection (or test for an internal mirror in an eclipsed rotamer) and find it is the (R,R)/chiral isomer, not meso — so Statement I's product does not match trans-but-2-ene. → Recall that alkaline KMnO4 adds two OH groups syn, so cis-but-2-ene gives meso-butane-2,3-diol. → Decode the second Newman projection and check it is indeed the meso (R,S) diol, then combine the two verdicts into the correct statement-pair option.
confusing options3 of 4 · options 1, 2, 4
Option 2 is what a candidate gets if he only checks the reaction type (anti for Br2, syn for KMnO4) without actually decoding the Newman pictures; option 4 is the mirror-image error — reading the dibromide as meso but mis-assigning the diol as the racemic one; option 1 requires both projections to be read correctly.
why it is trickyHard: two independent stereochemical analyses joined in one item — each needs a mechanism rule (anti vs syn addition) AND a Newman projection translated into meso-vs-chiral, and three of the four options are live because the common shortcut of trusting the reaction name alone lands squarely on option 2.
the catchNothing is signposted: the stem never says 'meso' or 'racemic', so the candidate must himself generate the R/S assignment from a staggered Newman drawing — the slowest manipulation in organic stereochemistry — and do it twice under time pressure; a single mis-read of which substituents belong to the front carbon flips the verdict.

why this chapterThe answer turns on the halogen chemistry of heating a halide with conc. H2SO4 and MnO2, where halide ions are oxidised to the halogen and MnO2 is reduced to Mn2+ (Group 17 preparation/reactions).
subtopic 1General Properties & Trends of Group 17 (Halogens)
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on halide ions (Cl-, I-) being oxidised to halogens by MnO2/conc. H2SO4 while Mn(IV) is reduced to Mn(II), a halogen chemical-property/preparation fact.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Given below are two statements:
In light of the above statements, choose the most appropriate answer from the options given below:
Statement-IHeating NaCl with concentrated H2SO4 and MnO2 results in oxidation of Mn.
Statement-IIHeating NaI with concentrated H2SO4 and MnO2 results in reduction of Mn.
  1. Statement-I is incorrect but Statement-II is correct.
  2. Both Statement-I and Statement-II are correct.
  3. Both Statement-I and Statement-II are incorrect.
  4. Statement-I is correct but Statement-II is incorrect.
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (1)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 1 · high
  1. Statement-I: 4NaCl + MnO2 + 4H2SO4 → MnCl2 + 4NaHSO4 + 2H2O + Cl2.
  2. Oxidation state of Mn changes from +4 in MnO2 to +2 in MnCl2, so Mn is reduced, not oxidised; Statement-I is incorrect.
  3. Statement-II: 2NaI + MnO2 + 3H2SO4 → 2NaHSO4 + MnSO4 + 2H2O + I2.
  4. Mn goes from +4 to +2 in MnSO4, so Mn is reduced; Statement-II is correct.
  5. Statement-I incorrect, Statement-II correct, which is option 1.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
11 highagree
difficultymedium · 2 steps
stepsRecall that in the halide + conc. H2SO4 + MnO2 reaction, MnO2 is the oxidising agent: Mn goes from +4 in MnO2 to +2 in MnCl2/MnSO4. → Apply that to Statement-I (NaCl) — Mn is reduced, not oxidised, so the statement is wrong — and to Statement-II (NaI) — Mn is again reduced, so the statement is right.
confusing options3 of 4 · options 1, 2, 3
Because the two statements are written as mirror images with the same reagents, a careless candidate assumes both must get the same verdict and picks 2 or 3; option 2 also attracts anyone who reads 'MnO2 + halide' as Mn being oxidised because the halide is oxidised, and only tracking Mn's own oxidation number (+4 to +2) separates 1 from its rivals.
why it is trickyOnly one concept is really at work — MnO2 acts as oxidant so Mn is always reduced — and once that is in hand both statements fall out at once, but the deliberately parallel phrasing keeps three options live and the 'both correct'/'both incorrect' pair is exactly the trap, so this is not an easy item.
the catchThe mirrored statement pair pushes candidates toward a single verdict for both; the key word is which species' oxidation state is being tracked — the halide is oxidised but Mn is reduced in both cases, and confusing the two lands squarely on option 2.

Q54Chemistry / inorganic

chapterClassification of Elements and Periodicity in Properties high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on the periodic trend of electron gain enthalpy down group 17 (Cl > F > Br > I, from the anomalously low value for F caused by its small size), which is taught under periodic trends in electron gain enthalpy.
subtopic 1Trends in Chemical Properties – Electron Gain Enthalpy
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on the halogen electron gain enthalpy order Cl > F > Br > I, with fluorine's anomaly due to its small size.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Among the following options, the correct trend in the electron gain enthalpy is
  1. I > Br > Cl > F
  2. F > Cl > Br > I
  3. Br > Cl > F > I
  4. Cl > F > Br > I
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (4)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 4 · high
  1. Down group 17 the atomic size increases, so the added electron is held less strongly: |ΔegH| falls from Cl to Br to I.
  2. F is an exception: its small, compact 2p subshell gives strong electron-electron repulsion, so |ΔegH|(F) < |ΔegH|(Cl) but still greater than Br.
  3. Order of negative electron gain enthalpy: Cl > F > Br > I, which is option 4.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
44 highagree
difficultymedium · 1 step
stepsRecall the halogen electron gain enthalpy order, including the anomaly that Cl releases more energy than F because of F's small size and high electron-electron repulsion in its compact 2p shell
confusing options2 of 4 · options 2, 4
Option 2 is exactly what a candidate gets by applying the plain group trend (most negative at the top, decreasing down), while option 4 is the same sequence corrected for the well-known fluorine anomaly — only knowing the exception separates them; options 1 and 3 scramble the order in ways no rule or exception produces and die on sight.
why it is trickySingle recall, but it is an inorganic EXCEPTION question: the trend-following answer is printed as a full option, so a candidate who learned only 'electron affinity decreases down the group' lands confidently on option 2, which is why this cannot be rated easy.
the catchThe fluorine anomaly — the trend-obedient distractor is the most attractive option in the set, and the stem gives no hint (no 'magnitude', no 'exception') that the anomaly is being tested.

Q56Chemistry / organic
subjectChemistry
streamorganic
branchnot applicable (Chemistry has no branches)
chapterBiomolecules high confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on protein secondary structure, where beta-pleated sheet chains are held together by intermolecular hydrogen bonds, which is taught under protein classification and structure in Biomolecules.
subtopic 1Proteins Classification and Structure
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on how protein secondary structure (beta-pleated sheet held by intermolecular hydrogen bonds) differs from alpha-helix, tertiary and quaternary structure.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
The correct statement about peptides and proteins is
  1. In α-helices, the polypeptide chain is twisted into a left-handed screw (helix) through intramolecular hydrogen bonds.
  2. Tertiary structure of proteins has two or more polypeptide subunits.
  3. Only the proteins having a quaternary structure are biologically active.
  4. In β-pleated sheet structures, peptide chains are held together by intermolecular hydrogen bonds.
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (4)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 4 · high
  1. Option 1: an α-helix is a right-handed screw held by intramolecular H-bonds, so 'left-handed' makes it false.
  2. Option 2: an arrangement of two or more polypeptide subunits is quaternary structure, not tertiary, so it is false.
  3. Option 3: proteins with only tertiary structure (e.g. monomeric enzymes, myoglobin) are biologically active, so 'only' makes it false.
  4. Option 4: in β-pleated sheets the chains are stretched out side by side and held together by intermolecular H-bonds, so it is true; the answer is option 4.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
44 highagree
difficultyhard · 4 steps
stepsCheck option 1 against NCERT: α-helix is a right-handed screw held by intramolecular H-bonds — the handedness word is wrong. → Check option 2: two or more polypeptide subunits defines quaternary, not tertiary structure. → Check option 3: biological activity is not restricted to quaternary-structured proteins (many monomeric enzymes are active). → Check option 4: β-pleated sheets are held by intermolecular hydrogen bonds between stretched-out chains — the printed line.
confusing options3 of 4 · options 1, 2, 4
Option 1 is the NCERT α-helix sentence with one word altered (left-handed for right-handed) and reads perfectly otherwise; option 2 is the adjacent definition — the quaternary-structure line offered as tertiary; option 4 is the key, and a candidate who only half-remembers 'hydrogen bonds hold protein structure' cannot separate 1 from 4 without recalling intra- versus intermolecular.
why it is trickyHard: four statements each needing its own recall check, and three of the four options stay live because two distractors are altered NCERT lines rather than nonsense, so elimination does not shortcut the four checks — each check is shallow, but a single misremembered qualifier (handedness, intra/inter, tertiary/quaternary) lands on a rival.
the catchClassic one-word-changed distractors: handedness in the α-helix line and tertiary-for-quaternary in the subunit line; the question turns on intra- versus intermolecular hydrogen bonding, a distinction a candidate who recognises the sentence but never read the qualifier will miss.

Q57Chemistry / organic
subjectChemistry
streamorganic
branchnot applicable (Chemistry has no branches)
chapterAldehydes, Ketones, and Carboxylic Acids; Amines high confidence
why this chapterThe answer needs both that p-nitrobenzoic acid (from KMnO4 oxidation) is a stronger acid than benzoic acid and that p-toluidine (from Sn/HCl reduction) is more basic than aniline, so it spans carboxylic-acid acidity and amine basicity.
subtopic 1Acidity of Carboxylic Acids
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicStatement-I, which decides the answer within this chapter, rests on the electron-withdrawing nitro group making p-nitrobenzoic acid a stronger acid than benzoic acid.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Given below are two statements:
In light of the above statements, choose the most appropriate answer from the options given below.
Statement-IOxidation of p-nitrotoluene with acidic KMnO4 gives an acid that is stronger than benzoic acid.
Statement-IIReduction of p-nitrotoluene with Sn/HCl followed by neutralization gives an amine that is more basic than aniline.
  1. Statement-I is incorrect but Statement-II is correct.
  2. Both Statement-I and Statement-II are correct.
  3. Both Statement-I and Statement-II are incorrect.
  4. Statement-I is correct but Statement-II is incorrect.
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (2)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · high
  1. Acidic KMnO4 oxidises the -CH3 of p-nitrotoluene to -COOH, giving p-nitrobenzoic acid.
  2. The -NO2 group withdraws electrons (-I, -R) and stabilises the carboxylate ion, so p-nitrobenzoic acid is stronger than benzoic acid; Statement-I is correct.
  3. Sn/HCl followed by neutralisation reduces -NO2 to -NH2, giving p-toluidine (p-methylaniline).
  4. The -CH3 group donates electrons (+I, hyperconjugation), raising electron density on N, so p-toluidine is more basic than aniline; Statement-II is correct.
  5. Both statements are correct, which is option 2.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
22 highagree
difficultyhard · 4 steps
stepsStatement-I: identify the product of acidic KMnO4 oxidation of p-nitrotoluene as p-nitrobenzoic acid (CH3 oxidised, NO2 untouched). → Compare its acidity with benzoic acid: the para -NO2 is electron-withdrawing (-I, -M) and stabilises the carboxylate, so the acid is stronger. → Statement-II: identify the product of Sn/HCl reduction then neutralisation of p-nitrotoluene as p-toluidine (NO2 -> NH2, CH3 untouched). → Compare its basicity with aniline: the para -CH3 is electron-donating, raising electron density on N, so it is more basic.
confusing options3 of 4 · options 1, 2, 4
Option 2 (both correct) is the honest route, but a candidate who slips on which group reacts — thinking the nitro is also attacked in the oxidation, or that the methyl is somehow lost/converted in the reduction — lands on 4 or 1, and both combinations are offered; only 3 is dead since at least one statement is plainly standard EWG/EDG reasoning.
why it is trickyFour independent checks — two product identifications plus two substituent-effect comparisons — and three of the four option combinations stay alive until all four checks are done, so elimination gives nothing and the whole question must be worked.
the catchBoth statements turn out true, which is the classic two-statement trap: a candidate who gets either product or either electronic effect backwards is handed a ready-made 'one correct, one incorrect' option rather than being warned by an absurd result. It is also an organic question hinging on which substituent the reagent condition touches (KMnO4 attacks the side chain, Sn/HCl the nitro group), not on naming the reaction.

chapterThermodynamics high confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on the entropy change of an ideal gas in isothermal free expansion (ΔS_sys = nR ln(V2/V1), ΔS_surr = 0 since no heat is exchanged), which is taught under Entropy in Thermodynamics.
subtopic 1Entropy, Second Law & Third Law
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on computing entropy change of the system (ΔS = nR ln(V2/V1) = 4.606R) and recognising that surroundings exchange no heat in free expansion, so ΔS_surroundings = 0.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Two moles of an ideal gas undergo free expansion from 10 L to 100 L at 300 K. The values of Δ Ssystem and Δ Ssurroundings are (R is universal gas constant)
  1. Δ Ssystem = 4.606R ; Δ Ssurroundings = 0
  2. Δ Ssystem = 0; Δ Ssurroundings = 0
  3. Δ Ssystem = 4.606 R; Δ Ssurroundings = -4.606 R
  4. Δ Ssystem = 0; Δ Ssurroundings = 4.606R
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (1)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 1 · high
  1. Entropy is a state function, so Δ Ssystem is found along a reversible isothermal path between the same states: Δ Ssystem = nRlnV2V1.
  2. Δ Ssystem = 2R × 2.303log1010010 = 2R × 2.303 = 4.606 R.
  3. In free expansion no heat is exchanged with the surroundings (q = 0), so Δ Ssurroundings = 0.
  4. Δ Ssystem = 4.606 R, Δ Ssurroundings = 0, which is option 1.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
11 highagree
difficultymedium · 2 steps
stepsRecognise that entropy is a state function, so ΔS_system for the isothermal volume change = nR ln(V2/V1) = 2R ln(10) = 4.606R → Recognise that free expansion is into vacuum: w = 0 and q = 0, so nothing is exchanged with the surroundings and ΔS_surroundings = 0
confusing options2 of 4 · options 1, 3
Option 3 is exactly what you get if you treat the expansion as a reversible isothermal one (surroundings absorb −q_rev/T, total ΔS = 0), which is the standard rival to the irreversible free-expansion answer; options 2 and 4 both put ΔS_system = 0, which is killed the moment the candidate writes nR ln(V2/V1).
why it is trickyTwo short steps, but once the log term 2R ln10 = 4.606R is computed the field collapses to options 1 and 3, and only the single fact that free expansion exchanges no heat with the surroundings separates them — one real rival, hence medium rather than easy.
the catchThe trap is treating the state-function calculation for the system as if the surroundings had supplied the heat reversibly; option 3 is tailored to that error, and option 2 catches anyone who argues q = 0 therefore ΔS = 0 for the system too.

subtopic 1Chemical Reactions: Reactions Due to Alpha Hydrogen
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on which products an aldol condensation (an alpha-hydrogen enolate reaction) can and cannot give, which is taught under reactions due to alpha hydrogen.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
The compound that CANNOT be obtained from the aldol condensation reaction shown below, is
  1. drawn by the pipeline from SMILES (RDKit) · CC1(C)CC/C(=C/c2ccccc2)C1=O C14H16O
  2. drawn by the pipeline from SMILES (RDKit) · CC1(C)CCC(C2=CCCC2(C)C)C1=O C14H22O
  3. drawn by the pipeline from SMILES (RDKit) · CC1(C)CC/C(=C\c2ccccc2)C1=O C14H16O
  4. drawn by the pipeline from SMILES (RDKit) · CC1(C)CC/C(=C2/CCCC2(C)C)C1=O C14H22O
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (2)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · medium
  1. In 2,2-dimethylcyclopentanone the α-carbon C2 is quaternary, so only C5 (CH2) forms the enolate.
  2. Cross aldol: the C5 enolate adds to PhCHO, and dehydration gives C5=CHPh, conjugated with C=O, as either the E or the Z isomer (options 1 and 3).
  3. Self aldol: the C5 enolate adds to the C=O of a second ketone, giving a β-hydroxy ketone with OH on the second ring's C1.
  4. E1cB dehydration removes the acidic α-H on C5 of the first ring, giving the exocyclic C=C between the two rings, conjugated with C=O (option 4).
  5. Option 2 keeps an sp3 α-CH and places the C=C inside the second ring, not conjugated with C=O, so aldol condensation cannot give it: option 2.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
22 mediumagree
difficultyhard · 4 steps
stepsIdentify the enolisable positions of 2,2-dimethylcyclopentanone: C2 is quaternary, so only C5 can form the enolate. → Write the crossed aldol with PhCHO followed by dehydration: benzylidene at C5, conjugated with the ring C=O, and note that both E and Z geometric isomers are drawn (options 1 and 3). → Write the self-condensation of the ketone: C5 enolate attacks the C1 carbonyl of a second molecule, dehydration toward C5 gives the cross-conjugated bis-ring enone of option 4. → Compare option 2, in which the exocyclic bond is single and the alkene lies inside the second ring, i.e. dehydration away from the carbonyl giving a non-conjugated alkene — not the aldol condensation outcome.
confusing options3 of 4 · options 2, 3, 4
Option 4 tempts anyone who assumes only the crossed product with PhCHO is possible and forgets self-condensation; option 3 tempts anyone who thinks only the E-benzylidene is formed and that the Z drawing is forbidden; option 2 is the real trap because it looks like a legitimate aldol-derived dimer and only the conjugation requirement in the dehydration step rules it out.
why it is trickyHard: four structural steps including recognising the blocked quaternary α carbon, generating both the cross and the self condensation products, and then judging four drawn structures against each other — and three of the four options have a genuine route a careless candidate would accept, so elimination by inspection fails.
the catchThe decision rests on the direction of dehydration (conjugated enone vs isolated ring alkene), a condition-driven organic point that is never stated in the stem, and the options are all closely related isomeric drawings that must be read carefully rather than recognised.

Q62Chemistry / physical
subjectChemistry
streamphysical
branchnot applicable (Chemistry has no branches)
chapterBasic Concepts of Chemistry high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on the rules for rounding off numbers to a given number of significant figures/decimal places (round-half-to-even when the dropped digit is exactly 5), taught under Significant Figures in Basic Concepts of Chemistry.
subtopic 1Significant Figures
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on the rounding-off rule for a terminal digit 5 (round to make the preceding digit even), which is taught under significant figures.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
The numbers 17.0145 and 21.0235 were rounded to three figures after the decimal point. The resulting numbers, respectively, are
  1. 17.015 and 21.024
  2. 17.014 and 21.023
  3. 17.015 and 21.023
  4. 17.014 and 21.024
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (4)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 4 · high
  1. NCERT rounding rule: if the digit to be dropped is 5 (followed by nothing), the preceding digit is left unchanged if it is even and increased by 1 if it is odd.
  2. 17.0145: the digit dropped is 5 and the preceding digit 4 is even, so 17.0145 → 17.014.
  3. 21.0235: the digit dropped is 5 and the preceding digit 3 is odd, so 21.0235 → 21.024.
  4. The pair 17.014 and 21.024 is option 4.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
44 highagree
difficultyhard · 2 steps
stepsRecognise that the digit to be dropped is exactly 5 with nothing after it, so the plain 'round up if 5' shortcut does not apply and the round-half-to-even convention of the significant-figures rules must be used → Apply it to both numbers: in 17.0145 the preceding digit 4 is even, so it stays -> 17.014; in 21.0235 the preceding digit 3 is odd, so it is raised to the next even digit -> 21.024
confusing options3 of 4 · options 1, 2, 4
Option 1 is exactly what the ordinary 'half rounds up' rule gives for both numbers and is the single most attractive wrong answer; option 2 is what simple truncation of the trailing 5 gives; option 4 is the round-half-to-even result, and the four options are deliberately built as all four combinations so no option can be struck off on sight.
why it is trickyOnly two short applications of a rule, but the options are the whole difficulty: they are the four possible pairings of 'up' and 'down' for the two numbers, so elimination is impossible and a candidate who uses the school 'five rounds up' habit lands squarely on option 1 without ever noticing anything was wrong.
the catchThe question turns on an unsignposted convention — rounding a bare 5 to the nearest even digit — that NCERT prints once in the significant-figures section and most candidates never internalise; nothing in the stem warns that the usual rule fails here.

subtopic 1Stoichiometry
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on the balanced combustion equation of n-butane (1 mol C4H10 gives 4 mol CO2), so 2 mol butane gives 8 mol CO2 = 352 g, a stoichiometric mass-mass calculation.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
The amount of carbon dioxide evolved upon complete combustion of 116 g of n-butane is (Given: atomic mass in amu H=1, C=12 and O=16)
  1. 362 g
  2. 352 g
  3. 322 g
  4. 176 g
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (2)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · high
  1. M(C4H10) = 4(12) + 10(1) = 58 g mol-1.
  2. n(C4H10) = 11658 = 2 mol.
  3. 2C4H10 + 13O2 → 8CO2 + 10H2O, so 1 mol butane gives 4 mol CO2.
  4. n(CO2) = 2 × 4 = 8 mol.
  5. m(CO2) = 8 × 44 = 352 g, which is option 2.

confusing options2 of 4 · options 2, 4
Option 4 (176 g) is exactly 4 mol of CO2, i.e. what you get if you treat 116 g as one mole of butane and skip the 116/58 = 2 division, so it is the standard careless landing spot; 362 g and 322 g correspond to no sensible route and are eliminated on sight.
why it is trickyFour steps with a live rival: the options do not give it away, because option 4 sits exactly on the dropped factor of two (one mole of butane instead of two), so the candidate must carry the mole conversion through all four steps to separate 352 g from 176 g; under the brief's rule (four or more steps, and not handed over by the options) this is hard, though each step is short to execute.
the catchThe single trap is the factor-of-two mole conversion (116 g is two moles, not one), and option 4 is precisely that error.

subjectChemistry
streamphysical
branchnot applicable (Chemistry has no branches)
chapterStructure of Atom high confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on counting radial nodes in a plot of the radial wavefunction against r, which is the treatment of atomic orbitals and their nodes.
subtopic 1Atomic Orbitals: Shapes, Energies and Nodes
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on identifying an orbital with two radial nodes from its radial wavefunction plot, which is taught under orbital shapes and nodes.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Consider the following schematic plots of orbital wavefunction (Ψr) against distance (r) from the nucleus. The figure representing two radial nodes in the orbital is
  1. D
  2. A
  3. B
  4. C
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (4)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 4 · high
  1. A radial node is a point r > 0 where Ψr changes sign, i.e. the curve crosses the dashed Ψr = 0 line.
  2. Plot A decays from a maximum without crossing zero: 0 radial nodes.
  3. Plot B crosses zero once (positive to negative): 1 radial node.
  4. Plot C crosses zero twice (positive to negative, then negative to positive): 2 radial nodes.
  5. Plot D starts at Ψr = 0 at r = 0 (not a radial node) and crosses zero once: 1 radial node.
  6. Two radial nodes corresponds to plot C, which is option 4.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
44 highagree
difficultymedium · 3 steps
stepsRecall that a radial node is a point where the radial wavefunction Ψ_r changes sign (crosses zero), not merely where the curve dips or flattens. → Count sign changes in each sketch: A decays monotonically (0 nodes); B goes below the axis once and returns asymptotically (1 crossing); D shows a hump, one dip below the axis and approach to zero (1 crossing). → Identify the plot with two zero crossings — the curve that falls below the axis, rises back above it, then flattens to zero — and match it to the lettered option.
confusing options2 of 4 · options 1, 4
C and D are near twins visually — both oscillate about the axis and both have one dip below it — and only careful counting of how many times the curve actually crosses the dashed zero line separates a one-node from a two-node plot; A (a plain monotonic decay, obviously 1s) and B (a single crossing) are discarded almost on sight.
why it is trickyMedium: the physics is one recalled definition, but the sketches are small and D is a genuine rival to C — a candidate who counts 'wiggles' or 'dips' instead of sign changes lands on D or even B, so the answer only comes out of a deliberate crossing-count on each of four tiny figures.
the catchThe whole item turns on one keyword — 'node' means a sign change of Ψ, not a minimum — and the C/D pair is drawn to punish the candidate who counts bumps instead of crossings.

Q65Chemistry / organic
subjectChemistry
streamorganic
branchnot applicable (Chemistry has no branches)
chapterGeneral Organic Chemistry high confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on carbocation stabilisation by hyperconjugation (filled C-H sigma) and resonance (filled benzene pi) donating into the empty p orbital, which is taught in General Organic Chemistry.
subtopic 1Hyperconjugation
subtopic 2Resonance Effect
why subtopic 1The answer depends on the empty p orbital of the 1-phenylethyl carbocation overlapping with filled C-H sigma orbitals of the CH3 group, which is hyperconjugation.
why subtopic 2The same answer equally requires the empty p orbital to be stabilized by the filled pi orbitals of the benzene ring, which is benzylic resonance stabilization.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
The following carbocation is stabilized by the interaction of the empty p orbital with
  1. empty σ* and empty π* orbitals
  2. filled σ and filled π orbitals
  3. empty σ and empty π* orbitals
  4. empty σ* and filled π orbitals
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (2)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · high
  1. The figure shows the 1-phenylethyl cation, C6H5+CH-CH3, with an empty p orbital on the cationic carbon.
  2. An empty orbital is stabilized only by donation from filled orbitals, so options with empty σ*, π* or σ donors are ruled out.
  3. The CH3 group gives hyperconjugation: filled σC-H orbitals overlap with the empty p orbital.
  4. The phenyl ring gives resonance: filled π orbitals of the ring overlap with the empty p orbital.
  5. Hence stabilization is by filled σ and filled π orbitals, which is option 2.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
22 highagree
difficultymedium · 3 steps
stepsRead the structure: a benzylic secondary cation (PhCH(+)CH3) with an empty p orbital. → Recognise that an empty p orbital is an electron ACCEPTOR, so whatever interacts with it must be a FILLED orbital. → Name the two donors: the filled C–H σ bonds of the adjacent CH3 (hyperconjugation) and the filled π orbitals of the benzene ring (resonance) — i.e. filled σ and filled π.
confusing options2 of 4 · options 2, 4
Option 2 is the correct donor pair, while option 4 is the classic mis-recall — students who remember hyperconjugation as 'σ-star involvement' (true for the σ -> σ* picture, or for anomeric donation INTO a σ*) pick 'empty σ* and filled π' without checking that an empty p can only accept, never donate.
why it is trickyOnly one real concept is tested, but the options are built so that the σ/σ* label decides the mark: options 1 and 3 die at once because an empty orbital cannot stabilise an empty p, leaving 2 against 4, and only the acceptor/donor argument separates them — a one-concept question with one genuine rival, hence medium not easy.
the catchThe whole item turns on one symbol — sigma versus sigma* — and the common classroom phrasing of hyperconjugation as 'sigma–sigma* overlap' pulls careless candidates straight onto option 4.

subtopic 1Werner’s Theory and Applications of Coordination Compounds
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on counting ionisable counter-ions (primary valence) outside the coordination sphere, so [Co(NH3)6]Cl3 gives one complex cation and three Cl- ions, which is taught under Werner's theory.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
A 1:3 electrolyte in an aqueous solution is
  1. [Co(NH3)3(NO2)3]
  2. [CoCl2(NH3)4]Cl
  3. [CoCl(NH3)5]Cl2
  4. [Co(NH3)6]Cl3
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (4)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 4 · high
  1. Only chloride outside the coordination sphere ionises in water; a 1:3 electrolyte gives one complex ion and three counter-ions.
  2. [Co(NH3)3(NO2)3] is neutral with no counter-ions, so it is a non-electrolyte.
  3. [CoCl2(NH3)4]Cl → [CoCl2(NH3)4]+ + Cl-, a 1:1 electrolyte.
  4. [CoCl(NH3)5]Cl2 → [CoCl(NH3)5]2+ + 2Cl-, a 1:2 electrolyte.

difficultyeasy · 1 step

confusing options2 of 4 · options 3, 4
Option 3 ionises to one cation and two chlorides and is the natural landing spot for a candidate who reads '1:3' as 'three ions in total' rather than one cation to three anions; option 1 is a non-electrolyte and option 2 a 1:1 electrolyte, both ruled out on sight.
why it is trickyEasy: one glance at what lies outside the coordination sphere settles it, and the four options form a clean graded series (0, 1, 2, 3 free chlorides) so only one rival survives at all — the misreading of the ratio notation.
the catchThe only trap is the notation '1:3' (cation:anion) being misread as total ion count, which lands exactly on option 3.

subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer comes from combining standard electrode potentials of related half-cells through their free energies (E° = 3(-0.04) - 2(-0.44) = +0.76 V), which is taught under standard electrode potential.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
The standard electrode potential (E°) for the half-cell reaction Fe3+ + e- → Fe2+ at 298 K is (Given: E°(Fe3+/Fe) = -0.04 V and E°(Fe2+/Fe) = -0.44 V at 298 K)
  1. +0.92 V
  2. +0.40 V
  3. +0.76 V
  4. -0.48 V
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (3)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 3 · high
  1. Target: Fe3+ + e- → Fe2+ = (Fe3+ + 3e- → Fe) + (Fe → Fe2+ + 2e-).
  2. Δ G1° = -3F(-0.04) = +0.12F.
  3. Δ G2° = -[-2F(-0.44)] = -0.88F.
  4. Δ G° = 0.12F - 0.88F = -0.76F = -1· F E°.
  5. E° = +0.76 V, which is option 3.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
33 highagree
difficultyhard · 4 steps
stepsRecognise that electrode potentials are not additive — convert each to ΔG° = -nFE°. → ΔG°₁ for Fe³⁺ + 3e⁻ → Fe = -3F(-0.04) = +0.12F. → ΔG°₂ for Fe²⁺ + 2e⁻ → Fe = -2F(-0.44) = +0.88F. → Subtract: ΔG° (Fe³⁺ + e⁻ → Fe²⁺) = ΔG°₁ - ΔG°₂ = -0.76F, then divide by -nF with n = 1 to get E°.
confusing options2 of 4 · options 2, 3
Option 2 (+0.40 V) is exactly what a candidate gets by subtracting the potentials directly, -0.04 - (-0.44), forgetting that only ΔG is additive; option 3 is the result of the correct n-weighted (Gibbs energy) treatment, and nothing in the stem signals which route is required.
why it is trickyFour short arithmetic steps, but the band is driven by the options: the single most common wrong route in this entire chapter — straight subtraction of E° values — lands precisely on option 2, so elimination is useless and only the ΔG = -nFE° reasoning separates the two live choices.
the catchThe unsignposted method choice (potentials are intensive, Gibbs energies additive) plus a distractor that is the exact output of the naive route; options 1 and 4 are mere sum/variant decoys, so the question is effectively a two-way test of one conceptual insight.

Consider the following statements about the solutions formed by mixing two liquids.
AAn ideal solution thus formed obeys Raoult's law throughout the composition range.
BMixture of chloroform and acetone shows negative deviation from Raoult's law.
CMixture of aniline and phenol shows positive deviation from Raoult's law.
  1. A and C only
  2. A and B only
  3. B and C only
  4. A only
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (2)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · high
  1. Statement A: an ideal solution obeys Raoult's law, pi = xi pi°, over the entire composition range, so A is true.
  2. Statement B: chloroform and acetone form a hydrogen bond Cl3C-H⋯O=C(CH3)2, making A-B attractions stronger than A-A and B-B, so the mixture shows negative deviation; B is true.

the catchThe sign of deviation for aniline–phenol is the single keyword the item turns on, and the wrong sign lands exactly on a printed option; statement A is a free giveaway that only kills one distractor.

Q70Chemistry / physical
subjectChemistry
streamphysical
branchnot applicable (Chemistry has no branches)
chapterElectrochemistry high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on the Debye-Huckel-Onsager relation for strong electrolytes (Lambda_m = Lambda_m0 - A*sqrt(c)) and Kohlrausch's law of independent migration of ions, both taught in Electrochemistry.
subtopic 1Variation of Conductivity and Molar Conductivity with Concentration
subtopic 2Kohlrausch Law of Independent Migration of Ions
why subtopic 1The answer depends on extrapolating Lambda_m = Lambda_m(0) - A*sqrt(c) for a strong electrolyte (145 = Lambda_m(0) - 90 x 0.1, giving Lambda_m(0) = 154), which is the concentration dependence of molar conductivity.
why subtopic 2The final step splits Lambda_m(0) = lambda(X+) + lambda(Y-) to get 154 - 74 = 80, which is Kohlrausch's law of independent migration of ions.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
For a salt XY, which is a strong electrolyte, the plot of Λm versus c has a slope of -90.0 S cm2 mol-3/2 L1/2 at 298 K. At 0.01 M concentration of XY, the value of Λm is 145.0 S cm2 mol-1. The limiting molar conductivity of Y- ion (λoY-, in S cm2 mol-1) at 298 K will be (Given : λoX+ = 74.0 S cm2 mol-1)
  1. 76.0
  2. 80.0
  3. 100.0
  4. 90.0
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (2)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · high
  1. For a strong electrolyte, Λm = Λmo - Ac, with slope -A = -90.0.
  2. c = 0.01 = 0.1.
  3. Λmo = 145.0 + 90.0 × 0.1 = 154.0 S cm2 mol-1.
  4. λoY- = Λmo - λoX+ = 154.0 - 74.0 = 80.0, which is option 2.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
22 highagree
difficultyhard · 4 steps
stepsRecall the Debye-Hückel-Onsager form Λm = Λ°m − A√c, with A the magnitude of the (negative) slope → Compute √c = √0.01 = 0.1 (mol/L)1/2 → Get Λ°m = 145.0 + 90.0 × 0.1 = 154.0 S cm² mol⁻¹ → Apply Kohlrausch's law: λ°(Y⁻) = Λ°m − λ°(X⁺) = 154.0 − 74.0
confusing options2 of 4 · options 2, 4
Option 2 is the correctly extrapolated value, while option 4 (90.0) simply echoes the given slope and catches a candidate who mis-handles the √c term or grabs a number straight from the stem; options 1 and 3 correspond to no clean wrong route and would mostly be guesses.
why it is trickyHard: four steps chaining two ideas from physical chemistry — back-extrapolating the Onsager plot to Λ°m and then splitting it by Kohlrausch's law — and the options do not give it away, since option 4 (the slope value itself) stays a live rival for a candidate who loses the √c factor, so the work has to be carried through to the end.
the catchTwo topics joined in one question (Onsager extrapolation plus Kohlrausch's law of independent migration), with the sign convention on the negative slope: the measured Λm must be increased, not decreased, by A√c; reversing that gives 136 − 74 = 62, which is not offered, but recognising √0.01 = 0.1 (not 0.01) is the real discriminator.

printed in the booklet

Arrange the following compounds in the increasing order of polarity. Choose the **correct** options given below. swer from the
ACH3CH2OCH2CH3
BCH3CH2OH
CCH3COCH3
DCH3COOH
  1. A < C < B < D
  2. A < B < C < D
  3. C < A < D < B
  4. C < A < B < D
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (1)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 1 · medium
  1. CH3CH2OCH2CH3 (A) has only a weak C-O-C dipole and no O-H, so it is the least polar.
  2. CH3COCH3 (C) has a polar C=O group but no O-H hydrogen-bond donor, so A < C.
  3. CH3CH2OH (B) has an O-H group that hydrogen bonds, so C < B.

confusing options3 of 4 · options 1, 2, 4
Options 1 and 2 are identical except that B and C are swapped — exactly the one comparison a candidate is shaky on (acetone's larger dipole moment versus ethanol's hydrogen-bonding O–H), and option 4 is live for anyone who reasons from dipole moment alone and puts the ketone lowest; only option 3, which ends with alcohol above acid, falls on sight.
why it is trickyOne short recall to plan but the elimination does not work: three of the four options survive a first pass, and the whole item turns on the single ketone-versus-alcohol placement that the two front-running options differ by, so it is a one-fact question with a genuine rival rather than an easy one.
the catchThe word 'polarity' is itself the trap — measured dipole moment ranks acetone above ethanol, while the NCERT-style ordering by overall polarity/H-bonding ranks ethanol higher, and that ambiguity is precisely what separates options 1 and 2.

In an acidic medium, 10 mL of 0.25 M oxalic acid is titrated with KMnO4 solution. If the volume of KMnO4 solution required to reach end point is 10 mL, the strength of the KMnO4 solution is
  1. 0.15 M
