Q75Chemistry / inorganic
subjectChemistry
streaminorganic
branchnot applicable (Chemistry has no branches)
chapterClassification of Elements and Periodicity in Properties medium confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on the anomalous behaviour of second-period elements: having only four valence orbitals (2s, 2p), boron's maximum covalency is four while heavier members like Al can expand it, which is taught under periodicity.
subtopic 1Anomalous Properties of Second Period Elements
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on second-period elements such as boron having only four valence orbitals (2s, 2p) and so a maximum covalency of four, which is taught under anomalous properties of second period elements.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
The correct statement is
  1. Aluminium has five valence orbitals.
  2. Boron has a maximum covalency of four.
  3. Beryllium has three valence orbitals.
  4. Magnesium has a maxium covalency of four.
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (2)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · high
  1. Boron is in period 2, so its valence orbitals are 2s and three 2p: 1 + 3 = 4 orbitals, giving maximum covalency 4 (e.g. [BF4]-). Option 2 is correct.
  2. Aluminium (period 3) has 3s, 3p and 3d valence orbitals: 1 + 3 + 5 = 9, not 5, so option 1 is false.
  3. Beryllium (period 2) has 2s and 2p: 1 + 3 = 4 valence orbitals, not 3, so option 3 is false.
  4. Magnesium (period 3) can use d orbitals, so its maximum covalency exceeds 4 (up to 6), so option 4 is false; the answer is option 2.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
22 highagree
difficultyhard · 4 steps
stepsCount aluminium's valence orbitals (3s + 3p + 3d = nine, or four if d is ignored) and reject 'five'. → Recall that boron, having only 2s and 2p (four orbitals) and no d, cannot exceed covalency four — the printed NCERT anomaly line. → Count beryllium's valence orbitals (2s + three 2p = four) and reject 'three'. → Recall magnesium can expand its octet (3d available), so its covalency is not capped at four.
confusing options3 of 4 · options 1, 2, 3
Options 1 and 3 are the same NCERT fact with the orbital count altered by one, so a candidate who half-remembers 'B has four valence orbitals' can be talked into 'Be has three' or 'Al has five'; option 4 is the clear outlier because magnesium's 3d orbitals are standard textbook material for octet expansion.
why it is trickyHard: four separate statement checks, and three of the four options are the same orbital-counting fact with a digit changed, so elimination fails on sight and each of options 1, 2 and 3 needs its own s + p (+ d) count before the boron anomaly line can be trusted as the key.
the catchAltered-number distractors: 'five' for aluminium and 'three' for beryllium are the printed valence-orbital counts shifted by one, and the candidate must actually count s + p orbitals rather than pattern-match; it is also an exception question (boron's capped covalency) where trend-following misleads.

2A k  B is a zero-order reaction, where k = 1.0 mol L-1 min-1. If the initial concentration of A is 2 M, then the time taken to complete 75% of the reaction will be
  1. 2.0 min
  2. 1.5 min
  3. 0.75 min
  4. 1.0 min
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (3)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 3 · medium
  1. For 2A → B, the rate of reaction is r = -12d[A]dt; for zero order r = k.
  2. So -d[A]dt = 2k = 2.0 mol L-1 min-1.
  3. Amount of A consumed for 75% completion: 0.75 × 2 M = 1.5 M.
  4. t = 1.5 M2.0 M min-1 = 0.75 min, which is option 3.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
33 mediumagree
difficultyhard · 3 steps
stepsFind the amount of A consumed: 75% of 2 M = 1.5 mol L⁻¹ → Write the zero-order rate law for 2A → B with the stoichiometric factor: rate = k = -12d[A]/dt, so [A] falls at 2k = 2.0 mol L⁻¹ min⁻¹ → t = 1.5 / 2.0 = 0.75 min
confusing options2 of 4 · options 2, 3

the catchThe classic lost factor of two: rate defined per stoichiometry (rate = -(1/2)d[A]/dt) versus rate = -d[A]/dt, with a distractor built for each convention.

subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)

printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
The correct order of solubility of the given salts in water at 298 K is
  1. Zn(OH)2 > AgBr > Hg2C12
  2. Hg2Cl2 > Zn(OH)2 > AgBr
  3. AgBr > Zn(OH)2 > Hg2C12
  4. Hg2Cl2 > AgBr > Zn(OH)2
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (1)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 1 · medium
  1. Read Ksp values from the table: AgBr: 5.0×10-13, Zn(OH)2: 1.0×10-15, Hg2Cl2: 1.3×10-18.
  2. AgBr (1:1): Ksp = s2 ⇒ s = 5.0×10-13 ≈ 7.07×10-7 M.
  3. Zn(OH)2 (1:2): Ksp = 4s3 ⇒ s = (2.5×10-16)1/3 ≈ 6.3×10-6 M.
  4. Hg2Cl2 ⇌ Hg22+ + 2Cl-: Ksp = 4s3 ⇒ s = (3.25×10-19)1/3 ≈ 6.9×10-7 M.
  5. Order: Zn(OH)2 > AgBr > Hg2Cl2, which is option 1.

stepsRecognise that solubility cannot be read off Ksp directly because the salts have different stoichiometry (AB vs AB2/A2B type) → AgBr (1:1): s = √(5.0×10⁻¹³) ≈ 7.1×10⁻⁷ M → Zn(OH)2 (1:2): Ksp = 4s³ → s = (1.0×10⁻¹⁵/4)^13 ≈ 6.3×10⁻⁶ M → Hg2Cl2 (Hg2²⁺ + 2Cl⁻, also 4s³): s = (1.3×10⁻¹⁸/4)^13 ≈ 6.9×10⁻⁷ M, then rank the three

why it is trickyHard: three separate solubility computations with two different Ksp expressions (√ for AgBr, cube root of Ksp/4 for both the 1:2 salts), and the decisive comparison is between AgBr (7.1×10⁻⁷) and Hg2Cl2 (6.9×10⁻⁷) — numbers so close that a sloppy cube root flips the order, while the obvious shortcut of ranking by Ksp lands straight on the rival option 3.
the catchThe near-tie between AgBr and Hg2Cl2 means the arithmetic must actually be carried out to two significant figures; and Hg2Cl2 must be recognised as dissociating into Hg2²⁺ + 2Cl⁻ (not 2Hg⁺), which is itself a point most candidates get wrong.

The correct decreasing order of oxidation state of the underlined atom in each molecule is

  1. Option 1: P4O6: 4x - 12 = 0 ⇒ x = +3; Cl2O7: 2x - 14 = 0 ⇒ x = +7; AlH3: Al = +3. Order +3, +7, +3 is not decreasing.

Q80Chemistry / physical
subjectChemistry
streamphysical
branchnot applicable (Chemistry has no branches)
chapterThermodynamics high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on the work done in reversible isothermal processes on an ideal gas (w = -nRT ln(Vf/Vi)) and the first law, which is taught in Thermodynamics.
subtopic 1Pressure–Volume Work for Isothermal Reversible Process
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe correct option rests on the work done in reversible isothermal steps 1 and 3, w = -nRT ln(Vf/Vi), with nR = 2 cal/K.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Consider the reversible processes for 1.0 mol of an ideal gas as shown in the figure. w1, w2, w3 and w4 represent work done (in calories) in the processes 1, 2, 3 and 4, respectively; Δ U2 and Δ U4 are changes in the internal energy for the processes 2 and 4, respectively. [use R = 2 cal K-1 mol-1] The correct option is
  1. w1 + w2 + w3 + w4 = 0
  2. w1 + w3 = -2T1 lnV2V1 - 2T2 lnV4V3
  3. w2 + w4 = Δ U2 - Δ U4
  4. w1 + w2 = 2T1 lnV2V1
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (2)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · high
  1. The figure is a Carnot cycle: processes 1 and 3 are reversible isothermals at T1 and T2; processes 2 and 4 are adiabatic.
  2. With w as work done on the gas, a reversible isothermal gives w = -nRTlnVfVi.
  3. w1 = -(1)(2)T1lnV2V1 = -2T1lnV2V1.
  4. w3 = -(1)(2)T2lnV4V3 = -2T2lnV4V3.
  5. w1 + w3 = -2T1lnV2V1 - 2T2lnV4V3, which is option 2.
  6. Check option 3: for adiabatic steps q=0, so w2 = Δ U2 = CV(T2 - T1) and w4 = Δ U4 = CV(T1 - T2) = -Δ U2; hence w2 + w4 = 0 ≠ Δ U2 - Δ U4 = 2Δ U2.
  7. Check option 1: over the cycle Δ U = 0, so ∑ w = -(q1 + q3) ≠ 0 since the cycle encloses non-zero area.
  8. Check option 4: w1 + w2 = -2T1lnV2V1 + Δ U2 ≠ 2T1lnV2V1; so option 2 is correct.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
22 highagree
difficultyhard · 5 steps
stepsIdentify the cycle: 1 and 3 are reversible isothermals (T1 and T2), 2 and 4 are adiabatics — i.e. a Carnot cycle. → Write w = -nRT ln(V_f/V_i) with R = 2 cal/K/mol for both isothermal legs, keeping the sign convention straight, and check option 2 term by term. → Test option 1: for the full cycle ΔU = 0 so q + w = 0, but w itself is the net (non-zero) cycle work — reject. → Test option 3: for adiabatics w = ΔU, so w2 + w4 = ΔU2 + ΔU4 (= 0 since the two temperature changes are opposite), not ΔU2 − ΔU4 — reject. → Test option 4: w1 carries a minus sign and w2 = ΔU2 is not zero, so the stated equality fails.
confusing options3 of 4 · options 1, 2, 4
Option 1 is the classic confusion of ΔU_cycle = 0 with w_cycle = 0; option 4 is exactly option 2's isothermal term with the sign convention flipped (and w2 wrongly dropped as zero); option 3 looks right to anyone who remembers w = ΔU for an adiabatic but does not notice the minus sign between the two ΔU terms.
why it is trickyHard: there is no single step — the candidate must independently evaluate four separate thermodynamic statements, and at least three of them are built from a correct formula used with one wrong sign, so elimination gives nothing and every option has to be worked.
the catchThe whole question turns on sign conventions (w = −nRT ln V2/V1, ΔU2 = −ΔU4) rather than on any new formula, and the wrong routes land exactly on options 1, 3 and 4; it is also long to execute because each option is a separate mini-derivation.

  1. [Ti(H2O)6]3+: Ti3+ is 3d1, so n = 1 and μ = 1(1+2) = 1.73 BM.

the catchLong to execute (four complexes, each a mini problem) and the decisive point is the spectrochemical-series exception-style judgement that CN- and NH3 force low spin while H2O does not; the high-spin Fe3+ answer with 5 unpaired electrons sits right there as option 3.

Q83Chemistry / physical
subjectChemistry
streamphysical
branchnot applicable (Chemistry has no branches)
chapterThermodynamics high confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on ΔG° = ΔH° − TΔS° = 0 at equilibrium (K = 1), so ΔS° = ΔH°/T = 666/333 = 2.0 kJ K⁻¹ mol⁻¹, a Gibbs energy and entropy relation taught in Thermodynamics.
subtopic 1Gibbs Energy and Spontaneity
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer uses ΔG° = ΔH° − TΔS° = 0 at equilibrium (K = 1), giving ΔS° = ΔH°/T = 666/333 = 2.0 kJ K⁻¹ mol⁻¹, which is the Gibbs energy–equilibrium relation.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
A protein undergoes reversible thermal denaturation from its state N to denatured state D according to N ⇌ D. At 60°C, the concentrations of both N and D are equal at equilibrium, and the standard enthalpy change of denaturation of 666 kJ mol-1. The standard entropy change (Δ S° in kJ K-1mol-1) of the protein upon denaturation at 60° C is closest to:
  1. 11.1
  2. 2.0
  3. 2000.0
  4. 333.0
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (2)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · high
  1. Equal concentrations of N and D at equilibrium give K = [D][N] = 1.
  2. Δ G° = -RTln K = -RTln 1 = 0.
  3. Δ G° = Δ H° - TΔ S° = 0 ⇒ Δ S° = Δ H°T.
  4. T = 60 + 273 = 333 K.
  5. Δ S° = 666 kJ mol-1333 K = 2.0 kJ K-1mol-1, which is option 2.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
22 highagree
difficultyhard · 3 steps
stepsRead '[N] = [D] at equilibrium' as K = 1 → From ΔG° = -RT ln K, conclude ΔG° = 0, so ΔH° = TΔS° → Convert 60 °C to 333 K and compute ΔS° = 666/333, keeping the answer in kJ K⁻¹ mol⁻¹ as asked
confusing options3 of 4 · options 1, 2, 3
Option 1 (11.1) is exactly 666/60, i.e. dividing by the Celsius temperature; option 3 (2000) is exactly the same division done with ΔH in joules, i.e. the right number in the wrong unit; option 4 (333) is just the Kelvin temperature and is the only dead option.
why it is trickyShort to execute — one division — but it needs an unsignposted inference (equal concentrations means K = 1, hence ΔG° = 0) before any formula can be used, and two of the three remaining options are the precise outputs of the two classic slips, Celsius-for-Kelvin and J-for-kJ, so elimination by plausibility fails and only correct work separates 2.0 from 11.1 and 2000.
the catchThe thermodynamic insight ΔG° = 0 at equal N and D is never stated, and both the temperature-unit and energy-unit traps are planted as exact option values.

subtopic 1Trends in Chemical Properties – Ionization Enthalpy
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on why the first ionization enthalpy of O is lower than N and F, namely that removing an electron from O gives a stable half-filled 2p configuration.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Given below are two statements : One is labelled as Assertion A and the other is labelled as Reason R. In light of the above statements, choose the most appropriate answer from the options given below:
Assertion AThe first ionization enthalpy of O is lower than that of N and F.
Reason RThe loss of an electron from O leads to stable half-filled p orbital.
  1. A is not correct but R is correct.
  2. Both A and R are correct and R is the correct explanation of A.
  3. Both A and R are correct and R is NOT the correct explanation of A.
  4. A is correct but R is not correct.
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (2)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · medium
  1. Configurations: N: 2s2 2p3, O: 2s2 2p4, F: 2s2 2p5.
  2. N has a stable half-filled 2p3 shell, so IE1(N) > IE1(O).
  3. F has higher effective nuclear charge, so IE1(F) > IE1(O); Assertion A is correct.
  4. O → O+ + e- gives O+: 2s2 2p3, a stable half-filled p subshell; Reason R is correct.

confusing options2 of 4 · options 2, 3

the catchClassic both-statements-true Assertion-Reason, where candidates score 40-50%; the keyword is 'N and F' — half-filled stability accounts for the N comparison only, and a candidate who stops at 'both true and the half-filled rule is the standard explanation' lands on the other of the two live options without ever testing the F half.

Q86Chemistry / organic
subjectChemistry
streamorganic
branchnot applicable (Chemistry has no branches)
chapterHydrocarbons high confidence
why this chapterThe answer turns on the stereochemistry of alkyne reduction: Lindlar's catalyst gives the cis-alkene K and Na/liq. NH3 gives the trans-alkene L, so K and L are geometrical isomers, which is taught under chemical properties of alkynes in Hydrocarbons.
subtopic 1Preparation of Alkenes
subtopic 2Isomerism
why subtopic 1The answer depends on the stereoselective partial reduction of an alkyne to alkenes, where Lindlar's catalyst gives the cis-alkene K and Na/liq. NH3 gives the trans-alkene L, which NCERT teaches under preparation of alkenes from alkynes.
why subtopic 2The options require classifying the relationship between the products (geometrical isomers vs enantiomers vs stereoisomers), which is the geometrical-isomerism content of the Isomerism topic.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Consider the following reaction sequences and choose the correct option.
  1. M and N are stereoisomers
  2. K and L are geometrical isomers
  3. K and L are enantiomers
  4. M and N are geometrical isomers
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (2)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · high
  1. Lindlar's catalyst gives syn addition: K = cis-Ph-CH=CH-CH3.
  2. Na/liq. NH3 gives anti addition: L = trans-Ph-CH=CH-CH3.
  3. K and L differ only in arrangement about the C=C, so they are geometrical isomers.
  4. K + HBr (ionic) goes via the benzylic carbocation: M = Ph-CHBr-CH2-CH3.
  5. L + HBr/benzoyl peroxide goes via the benzylic radical, Br on the other carbon: N = Ph-CH2-CHBr-CH3.
  6. M and N are position isomers, not stereoisomers or geometrical isomers; K and L are not enantiomers. Answer is option 2.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
22 highagree
difficultyhard · 5 steps
stepsLindlar/H2-Pd/C poisoned catalyst on Ph-C≡C-Me gives syn addition → K = cis (Z)-1-phenylpropene → Na/liq. NH3 gives anti addition → L = trans (E)-1-phenylpropene → Compare K and L: same connectivity, different double-bond geometry → geometrical isomers → K + HBr (ionic, Markovnikov via benzylic carbocation) → M = PhCHBr-CH2CH3 → L + HBr/benzoyl peroxide (radical, anti-Markovnikov via benzylic radical) → N = PhCH2-CHBrCH3; compare M and N → constitutional (position) isomers, not stereoisomers
confusing options3 of 4 · options 1, 2, 4
Option 2 is the clean cis/trans relation, but options 1 and 4 are both built on the trap that M and N differ only in stereochemistry — a candidate who ignores the peroxide effect gives both additions the same regiochemistry and then has to choose between 'stereoisomers' and 'geometrical isomers' for two saturated bromides.
why it is trickyFour or five linked steps across two sub-topics (alkyne partial reduction stereochemistry plus Markovnikov vs peroxide-effect regiochemistry), and three options stay alive to the end: the correct statement about K and L cannot be trusted until the M/N branch is also worked out, since a candidate who assumes M and N are stereoisomers will prefer option 1 or 4.
the catchThe whole question turns on the reaction CONDITION — Lindlar vs Na/NH3 for geometry, and HBr alone vs HBr/benzoyl peroxide for position — and the peroxide branch starts from the trans alkene, which tempts candidates into thinking the products differ only in geometry; it is also long to execute because four separate products must be drawn before any option can be ruled out.

Q87Chemistry / inorganic
subjectChemistry
streaminorganic
branchnot applicable (Chemistry has no branches)
chapterChemical Bonding and Molecular Structure high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on filling the molecular orbital energy-level sequence for Ne2 (20 electrons), whose last electrons occupy sigma*2p, which is molecular orbital theory.
subtopic 1Energy Level Diagram for Molecular Orbitals
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on the energy ordering and filling of molecular orbitals for Ne2 (20 electrons), which places the last electrons in sigma*2p as shown in the MO energy level diagram.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
The highest occupied molecular orbital of Ne2 is:
  1. σ*2p
  2. π2p
  3. σ2p
  4. π*2p
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (1)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 1 · high
  1. Ne2 has 2 × 10 = 20 electrons.
  2. For Z > 7 the order is σ1s < σ*1s < σ2s < σ*2s < σ2pz < π2px=π2py < π*2px=π*2py < σ*2pz.
  3. Filling 20 electrons: σ1s2 σ1s*2 σ2s2 σ2s*2 σ2pz2 π2px2 π2py2 π2px*2 π2py*2 σ2pz*2.
  4. The last orbital filled is σ*2p, so it is the HOMO, which is option 1.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
11 highagree
difficultymedium · 3 steps
stepsCount valence electrons in Ne2 (10 per atom, 20 total; 16 in the n=2 shell) → Write the MO filling order for a second-row homonuclear diatomic, noting that for Z>7 σ2pz lies below π2p and that σ*2pz is the topmost level → Fill right through σ2s, σ*2s, σ2pz, π2p, π*2p and into σ*2pz, then read off the last occupied orbital
confusing options2 of 4 · options 1, 4
Option 4 (π*2p) is exactly where a candidate stops if they forget that Ne2 has enough electrons to also fill the final σ*2pz, while option 1 is the key; options 2 and 3 are bonding orbitals that are filled long before the top and nobody who fills correctly would land there.
why it is trickyShort to execute but not a single recall: the candidate must know the full MO ordering for a diatomic beyond N2 and realise that with 20 electrons every orbital including the uppermost antibonding σ* is occupied, and the π*2p distractor catches anyone who stops one level early.
the catchThe σ2p/π2p ordering swap for Z>7 is a standard trap, and the real rival (π*2p) is the correct answer for a molecule with two fewer electrons — the question turns on the electron count, not on recognising orbital names.

subtopic 1Valence Shell Electron Pair Repulsion (VSEPR) Theory
subtopic 2Hybridisation
why subtopic 1The answer depends on predicting molecular geometry from electron pairs (PCl5 trigonal bipyramidal, BrF5 square pyramidal, BF4- tetrahedral), which is VSEPR theory.
why subtopic 2The square planar geometry of [Ni(CN)4]2- cannot be derived from VSEPR and rests on its dsp2 hybridisation, so hybridisation is a genuine second basis for the matching.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Match the species in List I with their geometry in List II
Choose the correct answer from the options given below:
APCl5ITetrahedral
BBrF5IISquare Planar
CBF4-IIITrigonal bipyramidal
D[Ni(CN)4]2-IVSquare pyramidal
  1. A-III, B-II, C-I, D-IV
  2. A-IV, B-III, C-I, D-II
  3. A-III, B-IV, C-I, D-II
  4. A-III, B-I, C-II, D-IV
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (3)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 3 · high
  1. PCl5: P has 5 bond pairs and 0 lone pairs, sp3d, so trigonal bipyramidal: A-III.
  2. BrF5: Br has 5 bond pairs and 1 lone pair, sp3d2, so square pyramidal: B-IV.
  3. BF4-: B has 4 bond pairs and 0 lone pairs, sp3, so tetrahedral: C-I.
  4. [Ni(CN)4]2-: Ni2+ is d8; strong-field CN- pairs the electrons, giving dsp2, so square planar: D-II.
  5. A-III, B-IV, C-I, D-II matches option 3.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
33 highagree
difficultymedium · 1 step
stepsBrF5 -- sp3d2 with one lone pair -> square pyramidal (IV); B-IV appears only in option 3, so this single call settles the match (PCl5 TBP, BF4- tetrahedral and [Ni(CN)4]2- dsp2 square planar are confirmations, not needed to separate the options)
confusing options2 of 4 · options 1, 3
Both start A-III and C-I and differ only in swapping square planar and square pyramidal between BrF5 and [Ni(CN)4]2-, which is exactly the AB5-with-a-lone-pair versus AB4-with-two-lone-pairs confusion plus the Ni(II)-CN hybridisation call.
why it is trickyOne decisive step -- each option gives BrF5 a different geometry, so knowing it is square pyramidal picks option 3 outright -- but option 1 is a real rival for the candidate who remembers only 'BrF5 is square-something', and only the lone-pair count on BrF5 separates them; options 2 (PCl5 square pyramidal) and 4 (BF4- square planar) die on sight.
the catchThe distractor swaps 'square planar' and 'square pyramidal' between BrF5 and the nickel complex -- a candidate who mixes up BrF5 (one lone pair) with XeF4-type square planar lands on option 1.

subtopic 1Chemical Reactions: Reactions Due to Alpha Hydrogen
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on the haloform reaction of acetophenone (formed by Friedel-Crafts acylation) with NaOCl, giving sodium benzoate (P) and chloroform (Q), which oxidises in air and light to poisonous phosgene; the haloform reaction of methyl ketones rests on halogenation of the alpha hydrogens.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
For the following reaction sequence, choose the correct option.
  1. Both P and Q are carbonyl compounds.
  2. If P is the sodium salt of a carboxylic acid, Q is a primary alcohol.
  3. P and Q are aromatic compounds.
  4. If P gives a carboxylic acid on acidification, Q gives a poisonous gas on exposure to air and light.
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (4)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 4 · high
  1. Friedel-Crafts acylation: C6H6 + CH3COCl AlCl3  C6H5COCH3 (acetophenone).
  2. Haloform reaction of the methyl ketone with NaOCl: C6H5COCH3 NaOCl  C6H5COONa + CHCl3, so P = C6H5COONa and Q = CHCl3.
  3. Acidifying P gives benzoic acid C6H5COOH, a carboxylic acid.
  4. Q in air and light: 2CHCl3 + O2 hν  2COCl2 + 2HCl, giving poisonous phosgene.
  5. Options 1, 2 and 3 fail (P is a carboxylate salt, Q is not an alcohol and is not aromatic), so option 4.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
44 highagree
difficultyhard · 4 steps
stepsRecognise step (i) CH3COCl/AlCl3 on benzene as Friedel-Crafts acylation giving acetophenone → Recognise NaOCl on a methyl ketone as the haloform reaction → Identify the two products: sodium benzoate (P) and chloroform (Q) → Test each of the four statements against those identities, including the fact that CHCl3 in air and light gives phosgene
confusing options3 of 4 · options 1, 2, 4
Option 2 is exactly what a candidate gets if he mistakes the step for a Cannizzaro-type disproportionation (salt + alcohol), option 1 is tempting because sodium benzoate does contain a C=O and a careless reader assumes the second fragment is also a carbonyl, and option 4 needs the extra chloroform-to-phosgene fact; only option 3 dies on sight once you see one product is CHCl3.
why it is trickyTwo linked reactions have to be run in order before any option can be judged, and then three of the four options survive the first glance -- the Cannizzaro-style distractor in option 2 is only killed by correctly naming the haloform products, so this is two topics (aromatic substitution plus haloform) joined with multiple live rivals.
the catchThe answer rests on a reaction CONDITION chain (acylation then hypochlorite, not oxidation or Cannizzaro) plus an off-reaction storage fact -- CHCl3 forming phosgene on exposure to air and light -- that is a separate line of NCERT from the synthesis itself; each option must be checked individually, making it long to execute as well.

stepsRecall plasmogamy = fusion of protoplasms (cytoplasms) of two gametes, karyogamy = fusion of the two nuclei — anchor A as II, which already kills options 1 and 4 → Recall meiosis in the diploid zygote/cell generates haploid spores — separates the two survivors (2 vs 3) instantly, since B cannot be meiosis
confusing options2 of 4 · options 2, 4
Option 4 is the exact mirror of the key — it swaps karyogamy and plasmogamy, the one confusion this question is built on — while options 1 and 3 both assign meiosis to a fusion event, which no candidate who has read the fungal life-cycle paragraph would accept.
why it is trickyEasy: a single NCERT definition pair carries the whole item, and the terms are self-translating (plasmo- = protoplasm, karyo- = nucleus), so anchoring A removes half the options and 'meiosis makes haploid spores' settles the rest without any further work.
the catchThe only real trap is the plasmogamy/karyogamy swap packaged as option 4; a candidate who has memorised the sequence as a phrase rather than by meaning can land there.

Which of the following statements regarding photorespiration are correct ? Choose the correct answer from the options given below :
aDo not occur in C3 plants
bCO2 is consumed and O2 is generated

The publisher's solutionprinted answer (3)

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 1 · high
  1. Photorespiration happens in C3 plants because RuBisCO binds O2, so (a) is false.
  2. In photorespiration O2 is consumed and CO2 is released, the reverse of (b), so (b) is false.

confusing options2 of 4 · options 1, 2

why it is trickyMedium: recall is direct NCERT, and rejecting (a) clears options 3 and 4 together, but option 2 survives as a real rival because (b) is the printed gas-exchange line with O2 and CO2 reversed, and only checking that direction separates it from option 1.
the catchAltered-line distractor: statement (b) reverses which gas is consumed and which released, and (a) swaps C3 for C4; both are one-word alterations of NCERT sentences.

solver reasoning: Photorespiration occurs in C3 plants (a false), consumes O2 and releases CO2 (b false), forms phosphoglycolate (c true) and synthesises no ATP or NADPH (d true), so (c) and (d) only.

Q117Biology / botany
subjectBiology
streamnot applicable (Biology has no streams)
branchbotany
chapterPhotosynthesis in Higher Plants high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on the historical order of early photosynthesis experiments (Priestley, Ingenhousz, Sachs, Engelmann), taught under Photosynthesis Experiments in Photosynthesis in Higher Plants.
subtopic 1Photosynthesis Experiments
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on the historical order of early photosynthesis experiments (Priestley's role of air, Ingenhousz's oxygen release, Sachs' glucose production, Engelmann's absorption spectra), which is taught under photosynthesis experiments.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Select the correct sequence of experiments that led to a gradual understanding of photosynthesis in green plants.
  1. Production of glucose → role of air → release of oxygen → absorption spectra of chlorophyll a and b
  2. Absorption spectra of chlorophyll a and b → production of glucose → release of oxygen → role of air
  3. Role of air → release of oxygen → production of glucose → absorption spectra of chlorophyll a and b
  4. Release of oxygen → production of glucose → absorption spectra of chlorophyll a and b → role of air
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (3)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 3 · high
  1. Priestley (1770) showed that air is restored by plants: role of air comes first.
  2. Ingenhousz then showed that green parts release oxygen in sunlight: release of oxygen is second.
  3. Sachs (1854) showed that glucose is produced (stored as starch) in green parts: production of glucose is third.
  4. Engelmann (1880s) obtained the action spectrum matching the absorption spectra of chlorophyll a and b: this is last.
  5. Order: role of air → release of oxygen → production of glucose → absorption spectra, which is option 3.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
33 highagree
difficultymedium · 2 steps
stepsMap each phrase to its experimenter: role of air = Priestley, release of oxygen in light = Ingenhousz, glucose/starch production = von Sachs, absorption spectra of chlorophyll a and b = Engelmann's action-spectrum work → Order the four by the chronology printed in the NCERT chapter and match to an option
confusing options3 of 4 · options 1, 3, 4
Option 4 is the trap for anyone who remembers Ingenhousz's oxygen release but forgets that Priestley's candle-and-mint 'role of air' experiment came first; option 1 is live for a candidate who pairs 'production of glucose' with the earliest textbook image; option 2 is nearly dead because everyone knows the chlorophyll absorption spectra came last, not first.
why it is trickyOnly one recall, but it is a four-item ordering and three of the four options are internally plausible orderings — the single fact that settles it (Priestley before Ingenhousz) is one line in the chapter, so elimination does not do the work for the candidate.
the catchThe whole question turns on which of two closely paired early experiments (role of air vs release of oxygen) came first; both options 3 and 4 differ only in that swap plus the placement of 'role of air', so a candidate who recalls the names but not the dates has a coin flip.

streamnot applicable (Biology has no streams)
branchboth
chapterCell Cycle and Cell Division high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on the events of meiosis: chromosomes are fully condensed by the end of prophase I (diakinesis), and it is meiosis II, not meiosis I, that resembles mitosis.
subtopic 1Prophase I and Its Types
subtopic 2Meiosis II
why subtopic 1Statement I is correct because chromosomes become fully condensed by diakinesis at the end of prophase I, which is taught under the substages of prophase I.
why subtopic 2Statement II is false because it is meiosis II, not meiosis I, that resembles normal mitosis, a fact taught under Meiosis II.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Given below are two statements : In the light of the above statements, choose the most appropriate answer from the options given below :
Statement IChromosomes are fully condensed at the end of prophase I.
Statement IIMeiosis I resembles mitosis.
  1. Statement I is incorrect, but Statement II is true
  2. Both Statement I and Statement II are true
  3. Both Statement I and Statement II are false
  4. Statement I is correct, but Statement II is false
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (4)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 4 · high
  1. Prophase I ends with diakinesis, by the end of which chromosomes are fully condensed and the spindle is assembled, so Statement I is true.
  2. Meiosis I is reductional (homologues separate); meiosis II separates sister chromatids and resembles mitosis, so Statement II is false.
  3. Statement I true and Statement II false matches option 4.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
44 highagree
difficultymedium · 3 steps
stepsRecall NCERT's diakinesis line: by the end of prophase I (diakinesis) chromosomes are fully condensed — Statement I is the printed line, so true. → Recall NCERT's line on meiosis II: 'Meiosis II resembles a normal mitosis' — Statement II has swapped II for I, so it is an altered line and false. → Match the true/false pair to the option combination.
confusing options2 of 4 · options 2, 4
Option 2 is exactly where a candidate lands who remembers the phrase 'resembles mitosis' without remembering which meiotic division it belongs to, since Statement I is genuinely true; option 4 needs the one-word check that it is meiosis II, not meiosis I, that resembles mitosis.
why it is trickyTwo independent NCERT recalls plus the combination step, and the options do not narrow anything — 2 and 4 agree on Statement I and differ only on the altered line in Statement II, so elimination is useless and the whole question rests on catching the swapped numeral.
the catchStatement II is the NCERT sentence with one character changed (meiosis II → meiosis I), the classic altered-line distractor; a candidate who recognises the familiar phrasing marks it true and picks option 2. Statement I also tempts the opposite error for anyone who thinks full condensation is only reached at metaphase, keeping option 1 faintly alive.

  1. A is not correct but R is correct
  2. Both A and R are correct and R is the correct explanation of A
  3. Both A and R are correct but R is not the correct explanation of A

Q142Biology / zoology
subjectBiology
streamnot applicable (Biology has no streams)
branchzoology
chapterPrinciples of Inheritance and Variation high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on Mendelian inheritance of the multiple ABO alleles (I^A, I^B, i): an I^A I^B parent cannot pass an i allele, so the child cannot be ii (group O).
subtopic 1Pleiotropy, Multiple Alleles & Polygenic Inheritance
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer depends on inheritance of ABO blood group alleles (I^A, I^B, i), the standard example of multiple alleles: a father I^A I^B cannot pass on i, so an O child is impossible.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
For a person with blood group 'O', which of the following is **not** a possible combination of parents' blood group genotypes?
  1. Father : IA IB and Mother : IA i
  2. Father : IA i and Mother : IB i
  3. Father : IA i and Mother : IA i
  4. Father : IB i and Mother : IB i
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (1)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 1 · high
  1. Blood group O has genotype ii, so the child must receive i from each parent.
  2. In option 1 the father is IAIB and has no i allele, so he cannot pass on i; an ii child is impossible.
  3. In options 2, 3 and 4 both parents carry i, so i × i → ii is possible (probability 14).
  4. Hence the combination that is not possible is option 1.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
11 highagree
difficultymedium · 2 steps
stepsRecall that blood group O is genotype ii, so each parent must pass on an i allele → Check each option for a parent with no i allele; only a father of I^A I^B cannot give i, so option 1 is the impossible combination
confusing options2 of 4 · options 1, 2
Option 1 is the key because the I^A I^B father carries no i; option 2 (A x B parents) is a real rival for a candidate who reasons at the phenotype level that A and B parents give A, B or AB children and forgets that both are heterozygous carriers of i.
why it is trickyMedium: the allele check is short, but option 2 pulls in a candidate who works from phenotypes instead of genotypes, and the 'not' in the stem must be held; options 3 and 4 (A x A and B x B heterozygotes) are seen at once to give ii and drop out.
the catchThe negative keyword 'not' together with a phenotype-level misconception (A x B cannot give O) that leads to option 2.

subtopic 1The Lac Operon

READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Which of the following statements about lac-operon is correct?
  1. Galactose can act as an inducer of lac operon
  2. Gene i is constitutively expressed
  3. Lactose activates repressor to bind to the operator
  4. Genes i, z, y and a share single common promoter
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (2)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · high
  1. Option 1: the inducer of the lac operon is lactose (allolactose), not galactose, so it is false.
  2. Option 2: the i gene codes for the repressor and is transcribed constitutively from its own promoter, so it is true.
  3. Option 3: lactose binds the repressor and inactivates it, preventing binding to the operator, so it is false.
  4. Option 4: only z, y and a share the common lac promoter; i has its own promoter, so it is false.
  5. Hence the correct option is 2.

22 highagree
difficultyhard · 2 steps
stepsRecall the lac operon layout: the regulator gene i has its own promoter and is transcribed continuously, while only the structural genes z, y and a sit under the shared lac promoter; check options 2 and 4 against this → Recall induction: allolactose (from lactose), not galactose, binds the repressor and inactivates it so it leaves the operator; check options 1 and 3 against this
confusing options3 of 4 · options 1, 2, 4
Option 1 is the NCERT inducer line with one word changed (galactose for lactose/allolactose), a sugar from the same pathway; option 4 is the 'z, y, a share a common promoter' line with i slipped in, and a candidate who pictures the operon as one block of four genes accepts it; option 2 is the printed line about the i gene.
why it is trickyTwo recall checks across all four statements, and two of the three distractors are NCERT lines altered by a single word (galactose for lactose, i added to z-y-a), so elimination fails and every statement must be checked against the exact printed fact; only option 3 (repressor 'activated' rather than inactivated) drops out at once.
the catchAltered-line distractors: option 1 swaps the inducer for a neighbouring sugar, and option 4 extends the shared-promoter statement to include the regulator gene i, which has its own promoter.

Q159Biology / zoology
subjectBiology
streamnot applicable (Biology has no streams)
branchzoology
chapterEvolution high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on the Hardy-Weinberg principle (frequency of AA = p^2 = 0.1^2 = 0.01), which is taught in Evolution.
subtopic 1Hardy-Weinberg Principle
subtopic 2not assigned (one subtopic chosen)
why this subtopicThe answer uses the Hardy-Weinberg relation that the frequency of AA equals p squared (0.1 squared = 0.01).
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
A population of diploid organisms is at Hardy-Weinberg equilibrium. If the frequency of allele A is 0.1, the frequency of AA is
  1. 0.99
  2. 0.01
  3. 0.02
  4. 0.10
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (2)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 2 · high
  1. At Hardy-Weinberg equilibrium the genotype frequencies are p2 : 2pq : q2, with f(AA) = p2.
  2. f(AA) = p2 = (0.1)2 = 0.01, which is option 2.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
22 highagree
difficultyeasy · 1 step
stepsApply the Hardy-Weinberg term p² for the homozygous AA frequency and square the given allele frequency: (0.1)²
confusing options2 of 4 · options 2, 4
Option 4 (0.10) is the allele frequency itself, live only for a candidate who confuses allele frequency with genotype frequency and forgets to square; option 2 is the squared value and the only number in the set that is any power of 0.1 other than 0.1 itself.
why it is trickyEasy: a single squaring of a given number using the first line of the Hardy-Weinberg chapter, and the options barely resist — 0.99 and 0.02 correspond to no sensible route (q² would be 0.81, 2pq would be 0.18, and neither is offered), so only the 'forgot to square' trap at 0.10 is even weakly live.

Only ciliary movement is of the same kind as the key (both are microtubule-based undulipodia), so a careless candidate who remembers 'hair-like beating' but not which organelle could pick it; muscular and amoeboid are a different kind of thing entirely (amoeboid belongs to leucocytes, muscular to tissues, not to a single cell's tail).
why it is trickyEasy: one recalled NCERT line, and two of the four options are not even mechanisms available to a sperm cell, while the remaining rival (ciliary) is separated by the single most-printed fact about sperm structure — the tail is a flagellum.

  1. Plasmids have their own origin of replication and replicate independently of chromosomal DNA, so Statement I is correct.

Q176Biology / botany
subjectBiology
streamnot applicable (Biology has no streams)
branchbotany
chapterEcosystem high confidence
why this chapterThe answer depends on facts about energy flow and ecological pyramids (unidirectional energy flow, upright energy pyramid, 10% law of energy transfer), which are taught in Ecosystem.
subtopic 1Energy Flow
subtopic 2Ecological Pyramids
why subtopic 1The answer depends on energy flow being unidirectional and following the 10% law (not 1%), which is taught under energy flow.
why subtopic 2Statement (b), that the pyramid of energy can never be inverted, is a fact about ecological pyramids and is one of the two statements the correct option keeps.
printed in the booklet
READ FROM THE IMAGE by b3-question-reader
Which of the following statements are correct? Choose the correct answer from the options given below:
(a)Energy flow from producers to consumers is unidirectional
(b)Energy pyramid can never be inverted
(c)Transfer of energy follows the 1% law
  1. (b) and (c) only
  2. (a), (b) and (c)
  3. (a) and (b) only
  4. (a) and (c) only
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (3)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 3 · high
  1. Energy flows one way, from producers to consumers (sun → producers → consumers), so statement (a) is correct.
  2. Each trophic level holds less energy than the level below it, so the energy pyramid is always upright and can never be inverted; statement (b) is correct.
  3. Energy transfer between trophic levels follows Lindeman's 10% law, not a 1% law, so statement (c) is incorrect.
  4. Only (a) and (b) are correct, which is option 3.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
33 highagree
difficultymedium · 3 steps
stepsCheck (a): energy flow in an ecosystem is unidirectional — true per NCERT. → Check (b): the pyramid of energy is always upright and can never be inverted — true (contrast with pyramid of biomass in a sea, which can be inverted). → Check (c): transfer between trophic levels follows the 10% law, not 1% — false; then match the true set (a)+(b) to the option list.
confusing options2 of 4 · options 3, 4
Option 3 is the correct combination; option 4 is live for the candidate who remembers 'the inverted pyramid in a sea' and wrongly rejects (b), not noticing that the inversion applies to biomass and not to energy.
why it is trickyThree statements each needing its own verification, and the real work is the near-neighbour trap in (b) — pyramid of energy versus pyramid of biomass — which is exactly the confusion option 4 is built on; options 1 and 2 are nearly dead because '1% law' is a blatantly altered form of the printed 10% law that most prepared candidates spot instantly, so elimination narrows it to a straight 3-versus-4 decision.
the catchTwo NCERT lines altered by a single element: '1%' for '10%', and 'energy pyramid' standing where 'biomass pyramid' would make the inversion claim false — the second one is the discriminating item.

During PCR, primers bind to the DNA strands in the ________ step.
  1. ligation
  2. denaturation
  3. extension
  4. annealing

  1. PCR cycle: denaturation separates strands, annealing lets primers bind to the template, extension lengthens them; so primers bind in annealing, option 4.
printed in the bookletsolved independentlyverdict
44 highagree
difficultyeasy · 1 step

confusing options1 of 4 · option 4

why it is trickyEasy: one recalled NCERT fact, and the options narrow it at once. Ligation is not a PCR step, and denaturation plainly comes before any primer is involved. Extension is the only near neighbour, and the stem's 'bind' rules it out for any prepared candidate.
the catchExtension is the near-neighbour step. A careless candidate who links primers with polymerase extension could pick it.

Given below are two statements:
In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer from the options given below:
Statement IDown's syndrome is caused by the absence of one of the X-chromosomes.
Statement IITurner's syndrome is caused by the presence of an additional copy of the chromosomes.
  1. Statement I is incorrect but Statement II is correct
  2. Both Statement I and Statement II are correct
  3. Both Statement I and Statement II are incorrect
  4. Statement I is correct but Statement II is incorrect
The publisher's solutionprinted answer (3)

no worked solution is printed for this question

Solved independently (answer withheld)AI: 3 · high
  1. Down's syndrome is caused by an additional copy of chromosome 21 (trisomy 21), not by absence of an X chromosome, so Statement I is incorrect.
  2. Turner's syndrome is caused by the absence of one X chromosome (44 + XO, i.e. 45, X0), not by an additional chromosome, so Statement II is incorrect.
  3. Both statements are incorrect, which is option 3.

confusing options1 of 4 · option 3
The two statements simply swap the textbook causes of Down's and Turner's syndromes, so once either swap is spotted the other is obvious and only the 'both incorrect' option survives; options 1, 2 and 4 each need one of the swapped lines to read as true.
why it is trickyEasy: two one-line NCERT recalls about the most-taught chromosomal disorders, and because the statements are a plain swap of each other's causes rather than a one-word alteration, spotting one error exposes the other and the options settle it at once.
